  3. to each number the student with that number
  4. to each student their ID number
Explanation. A function assigns to EVERY element of the domain EXACTLY ONE element of the codomain. “One of their marks” is not unique; the third option goes the opposite way.
am1-f-872 · level facile · notes
A function $f: A \rr B$ with $A = \{1, 2, 3\}$ is given by the table $f(1) = 5$, $f(2) = 5$, $f(3) = 7$. Which statement is correct?
  1. it is not a function, because $5$ appears twice
  2. it is a function: different elements may have the same value
  3. it is a function only if $B = \{5, 7\}$
  4. it is not a function, because $7$ has a single preimage
Explanation. The definition requires one value for each argument, not different values for different arguments: $f$ is a function (not injective). The codomain may contain $\{5, 7\}$ and be larger.
am1-f-873 · level facile · notes
What is the natural domain of the rule $f(x) = \frac{\sqrt{x}}{x - 4}$?
  1. $[0, 4) \cup (4, +\infty)$
  2. $[0, +\infty)$
  3. $(0, 4) \cup (4, +\infty)$
  4. $\R \setminus \{4\}$
Explanation. We need $x \ge 0$ for the root and $x \neq 4$ for the denominator; $x = 0$ is allowed since $\frac{0}{-4} = 0$.
am1-f-874 · level ipotesi · notes
Two functions are equal if they have the same domain, the same codomain and the same rule. Are $f(x) = \frac{x^2 - 1}{x - 1}$ (on its natural domain) and $g(x) = x + 1$ (on $\R$) equal?
  1. yes, because $\frac{x^2 - 1}{x - 1} = x + 1$
  2. yes, because they have the same graph
  3. no: $f$ is not defined at $x = 1$, while $g$ is
  4. no, because $f(0) \neq g(0)$
Explanation. The rules agree for $x \neq 1$, but the domains differ ($\R \setminus \{1\}$ and $\R$): the graph of $f$ is the line without the point $(1, 2)$. $f(0) = 1 = g(0)$.
am1-f-875 · level facile · notes
Let $f: \R \rr \R$ be a function. Which statement follows from the definition of function?
  1. if $f(a) = f(b)$ then $a = b$
  2. if $a = b$ then $f(a) = f(b)$
  3. every $y \in \R$ is the value of some $x$
  4. $f$ has an inverse
Explanation. Each argument has only one value, so equal arguments give equal values. The first option is injectivity, the third surjectivity: neither is required.
am1-f-876 · level facile · notes
For $f: \R \rr \R$, $f(x) = x^2$, what is the image $f([-1, 2])$ of the interval $[-1, 2]$?
  1. $[1, 4]$
  2. $[-1, 4]$
  3. $[-1, 2]$
  4. $[0, 4]$
Explanation. $x^2$ equals $0$ at $x = 0 \in [-1, 2]$ and at most $4$ at $x = 2$; it takes all values in between. Computing only $f(-1) = 1$ and $f(2) = 4$ misses the minimum.
am1-f-877 · level ipotesi · notes
The inverse $f^{-1}$ is defined for a bijection. Why does $f: \R \rr \R$, $f(x) = x^2$ have no inverse, and which restriction makes it invertible?
  1. it is not injective; on $[0, +\infty)$ it is, inverse $\sqrt{y}$
  2. it is not continuous; restricting it to $\Z$ is enough
  3. it is not injective but it is surjective; restricting the codomain is enough
  4. it has inverse $\pm\sqrt{y}$
Explanation. $f(-1) = f(1)$ (not injective) and $-1$ is not a value (not surjective). On $[0, +\infty)$ with codomain $[0, +\infty)$ it is bijective; $\pm\sqrt{y}$ is not a function.
am1-f-878 · level facile · notes
Which function is surjective (every element of the codomain is a value)?
  1. $f: \R \rr \R$, $f(x) = e^x$
  2. $f: \R \rr \R$, $f(x) = x^2$
  3. $f: \R \rr [-1, 1]$, $f(x) = \sin x$
  4. $f: \N \rr \N$, $f(n) = 2n$
Explanation. $\sin$ takes every value in $[-1, 1]$. $e^x$ and $x^2$ take no negative values, $2n$ takes no odd ones.
am1-f-927 · level teorema · notes
Let $f: A \rr B$ be a bijection and $f^{-1}: B \rr A$ its inverse. Which equalities hold?
  1. $f^{-1}(f(a)) = f(a)$ for every $a \in A$
  2. $f^{-1} \circ f$ and $f \circ f^{-1}$ are identities
  3. $f^{-1}(b) = \frac{1}{f(b)}$ for every $b \in B$
  4. $f(f^{-1}(a)) = a$ only for $a \in A \cap B$
Explanation. The inverse maps $b$ to the unique $a$ with $f(a) = b$: the two compositions are the identities of $A$ and of $B$. $\frac{1}{f}$ is the reciprocal, a different thing.
am1-f-928 · level facile · notes
Let $f: \{1, 2, 3\} \rr \{a, b, c\}$ be the bijection with $f(1) = c$, $f(2) = a$, $f(3) = b$. What are $f^{-1}(a)$ and $f^{-1}(c)$?
  1. $f^{-1}(a) = 1$, $f^{-1}(c) = 3$
  2. $f^{-1}(a) = c$, $f^{-1}(c) = b$
  3. they are undefined
  4. $f^{-1}(a) = 2$, $f^{-1}(c) = 1$
Explanation. $f^{-1}(y)$ is the element that $f$ maps to $y$: $f(2) = a$ and $f(1) = c$.

3. Real functions of a real variable (26)

am1-f-038 · level facile · notes
Which function is odd?
  1. $f(x) = x^2 + x$
  2. $f(x) = x^4 - 1$
  3. $f(x) = x^3 - x$
  4. $f(x) = x^3 + 1$
Explanation. $f(-x) = -x^3 + x = -f(x)$: the graph is symmetric with respect to the origin. $x^4 - 1$ is even, the others are neither even nor odd.
am1-f-039 · level facile · notes
What is the period of the function $f(x) = \sin(3x)$?
  1. $\frac{2\pi}{3}$
  2. $6\pi$
  3. $\frac{\pi}{3}$
  4. $2\pi$
Explanation. $\sin(3(x + T)) = \sin(3x + 3T)$ and the smallest $T > 0$ with $3T = 2\pi$ is $T = \frac{2\pi}{3}$.
am1-f-040 · level facile · notes
With the convention that “increasing” means strictly increasing, $f$ is increasing on the interval $I$ if for every $x_1, x_2 \in I$:
  1. $x_1 < x_2 \Rightarrow f(x_1) \le f(x_2)$
  2. $x_1 < x_2 \Rightarrow f(x_1) < f(x_2)$
  3. $x_1 < x_2 \Rightarrow f(x_1) > f(x_2)$
  4. $f(x_1) \le f(x_2) \Rightarrow x_1 < x_2$
Explanation. “Increasing” is meant in the strict sense; with $\le$ one gets the definition of a non-decreasing function.
am1-f-204 · level facile · notes
Where is the function $f(x) = \frac{x - 2}{x + 1}$ positive?
  1. $(-1, 2)$
  2. $(2, \ip)$
  3. $(-\infty, -1) \cup (2, \ip)$
  4. $(-\infty, -1] \cup [2, \ip)$
Explanation. The quotient is positive when numerator and denominator have the same sign: both positive for $x > 2$, both negative for $x < -1$.
am1-f-205 · level facile · notes
Which function, defined on all of $\R$, is bounded?
  1. $f(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}$
  2. $f(x) = x^2$
  3. $f(x) = e^x$
  4. $f(x) = x \sin x$
Explanation. $0 < \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \le 1$ for every $x$. $x^2$ and $e^x$ are not bounded above; $x\sin x$ takes arbitrarily large values (e.g. at $x = \frac\pi2 + 2k\pi$).
am1-f-206 · level facile · notes
If $f$ and $g$ are odd functions defined on $\R$, the product $f \cdot g$ is:
  1. odd
  2. neither even nor odd in general
  3. identically zero
  4. even
Explanation. $(fg)(-x) = f(-x)\,g(-x) = (-f(x))(-g(x)) = f(x)\,g(x)$: the product is even, like $x \cdot x^3 = x^4$.
am1-f-207 · level facile · notes
On which interval is the function $f(x) = x^2 - 4x$ (strictly) decreasing?
  1. $[2, \ip)$
  2. $(-\infty, 2]$
  3. $(-\infty, 0]$
  4. $[0, 4]$
Explanation. $f(x) = (x - 2)^2 - 4$ is a parabola with vertex at $x = 2$ opening upward: it decreases to the left of the vertex.
am1-f-208 · level facile · notes
What is the (minimal) period of the function $f(x) = \sin x + \cos(2x)$?
  1. $\pi$
  2. $4\pi$
  3. $2\pi$
  4. $\frac{\pi}{2}$
Explanation. $\sin x$ has period $2\pi$ and $\cos(2x)$ period $\pi$: the sum has their least common multiple, $2\pi$. With $T = \pi$: $f(x + \pi) = -\sin x + \cos 2x \neq f(x)$.
am1-f-492 · level facile · notes
The graph of a real function $f: D \rr \R$, with $D \subseteq \R$, is the set:
  1. $\{(x, f(x)) : x \in D\}$
  2. $\{(f(x), x) : x \in D\}$
  3. $\{f(x) : x \in D\}$
  4. $\{(x, y) : x \in D,~ y \le f(x)\}$
Explanation. The graph consists of the pairs (abscissa, value). The second option is the graph reflected in $y = x$, the third is the image, the fourth the region below the graph.
am1-f-493 · level facile · notes
Which curve in the plane is NOT the graph of a function $y = f(x)$?
  1. the parabola $y = x^2 - 1$
  2. the line $y = 3$
  3. the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1$
  4. the curve $y = |x|$
Explanation. A vertical line meets the graph of a function at most once. The line $x = 0$ meets the circle at the two points $(0, \pm 1)$.
am1-f-494 · level facile · notes
Where is the function $f(x) = x\,(x - 3)^2$ (strictly) negative?
  1. $(0, 3)$
  2. $(-\infty, 0)$
  3. $(-\infty, 0) \cup (0, 3)$
  4. $(-\infty, 3)$
Explanation. The factor $(x - 3)^2$ is $\ge 0$ and vanishes only at $3$: the sign is that of $x$. Hence $f(x) < 0$ exactly for $x < 0$.
am1-f-495 · level facile · notes
Which function $f: \R \rr \R$ is bounded above but not bounded below?
  1. $f(x) = x^2 - 4x$
  2. $f(x) = \sin x$
  3. $f(x) = x^3$
  4. $f(x) = -e^{x}$
Explanation. $-e^x < 0$ for every $x$ and $-e^x \rr \im$ as $x \rr \ip$. $x^2 - 4x \ge -4$ is bounded below, $\sin x$ is bounded, $x^3$ is bounded neither above nor below.
am1-f-879 · level facile · notes
A real function of a real variable is $f: D \rr \R$ with $D \subseteq \R$. What is the largest $D$ on which the rule $f(x) = \log(4 - x^2)$ (natural $\log$) defines such a function?
  1. $(-2, 2)$
  2. $[-2, 2]$
  3. $(-\infty, -2) \cup (2, +\infty)$
  4. $(0, 2)$
Explanation. We need $4 - x^2 > 0$, i.e. $-2 < x < 2$; the endpoints give $\log 0$, undefined.
am1-f-880 · level facile · notes
The vertical line $x = 3$ meets the graph of a real function $f: D \rr \R$. In how many points at most?
  1. in infinitely many points
  2. in two points
  3. in at most one point
  4. in exactly one point
Explanation. The graph consists of the points $(x, f(x))$ and each $x$ has a single value: one point if $3 \in D$, none otherwise.
am1-f-881 · level facile · notes
Where is the function $f(x) = \frac{x^2 - 9}{x}$ non-negative ($f(x) \ge 0$)?
  1. $[-3, 3]$
  2. $(-\infty, -3] \cup [3, +\infty)$
  3. $[3, +\infty)$
  4. $[-3, 0) \cup [3, +\infty)$
Explanation. Numerator $\ge 0$ for $|x| \ge 3$, denominator $> 0$ for $x > 0$ ($x = 0$ excluded). The quotient is $\ge 0$ on $[-3, 0)$ (numerator $\le 0$, denominator $< 0$) and on $[3, +\infty)$.
am1-f-882 · level teorema · notes
Sign of a function: if $f > 0$ and $g < 0$ on a set $I$, what are the signs of $f\,g$ and $\frac{f}{g}$ on $I$?
  1. $f\,g > 0$ and $\frac{f}{g} < 0$
  2. both $< 0$
  3. both $> 0$
  4. the sign depends on the values
Explanation. By the rule of signs the product and the quotient of a positive and a negative number are negative; the sign of $f + g$ instead may depend on the values.
am1-f-883 · level ipotesi · notes
A function is bounded on $I$ if there is $M$ with $|f(x)| \le M$ for every $x \in I$. The function $f(x) = \frac1x$ is bounded on $[1, +\infty)$; is it also bounded on $(0, +\infty)$?
  1. yes, with the same constant $M = 1$
  2. yes, because it is decreasing
  3. no, it is unbounded near $0$
  4. no, because it is not defined at $0$
Explanation. Boundedness depends on the set: on $(0, 1)$ we have $\frac1x > M$ for $x < \frac1M$. Not being defined at $0$ is not the reason: the set $(0, +\infty)$ does not contain $0$ anyway.
am1-f-884 · level facile · notes
What is the image of the function $f(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}$, defined on $\R$, and is the function bounded?
  1. $(0, 1]$: it is bounded
  2. $[0, 1]$: it is bounded
  3. $(0, +\infty)$: it is not bounded
  4. $(0, 1)$: it is bounded
Explanation. $1 + x^2 \ge 1$, so $0 < f(x) \le 1$, with $f(0) = 1$; the value $0$ is never taken.
am1-f-885 · level facile · notes
A function $f$ with symmetric domain is even if $f(-x) = f(x)$. Which function is even?
  1. $f(x) = x^3 + x$
  2. $f(x) = x\sin x$
  3. $f(x) = x + \cos x$
  4. $f(x) = e^x$
Explanation. $(-x)\sin(-x) = x\sin x$: a product of two odd functions. $x^3 + x$ is odd, the others are neither even nor odd.
am1-f-886 · level ipotesi · notes
The definition of an even function requires a domain symmetric about $0$. Is the function $f(x) = x^2$ defined on $D = [-1, 2]$ even?
  1. yes, because $(-x)^2 = x^2$
  2. yes, because its graph is a parabola
  3. no, it is odd
  4. no: for $x = 2 \in D$ the point $-2$ is not in $D$
Explanation. Symmetry of the domain is part of the definition: on $[-1, 2]$ one cannot compare $f(2)$ with $f(-2)$.
am1-f-887 · level teorema · notes
Let $f: \R \rr \R$ be an odd function ($f(-x) = -f(x)$). Which property necessarily holds?
  1. $f(0) = 1$
  2. $f$ is increasing
  3. $f(0) = 0$
  4. $f(x) \ge 0$ for $x \ge 0$
Explanation. With $x = 0$: $f(0) = -f(0)$, so $f(0) = 0$. $-x$ is odd and decreasing; $-x$ is also negative for $x > 0$.
am1-f-888 · level facile · notes
With the conventions of the notes (“increasing” = strictly), which function is non-decreasing on $\R$ but NOT increasing?
  1. $f(x) = x^3$
  2. $f(x) = e^x$
  3. $f(x) = -x$
  4. $f(x) = [x]$
Explanation. The integer part satisfies $x_1 < x_2 \Rightarrow [x_1] \le [x_2]$ but is constant on each $[n, n+1)$. $x^3$ and $e^x$ are increasing, $-x$ is decreasing.
am1-f-889 · level ipotesi · notes
A strictly increasing function is injective. Does the same hold for a function that is only non-increasing on $\R$?
  1. yes, every monotone function is injective
  2. no: a constant function is non-increasing but not injective
  3. yes, if it is also bounded
  4. no, because non-increasing functions are not monotone
Explanation. With only the weak inequality two different points may have the same value; strict monotonicity is needed for injectivity.
am1-f-890 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ and $g$ be (strictly) increasing on $\R$. Which function is certainly increasing on $\R$?
  1. $f + g$
  2. $f \cdot g$
  3. $f - g$
  4. $\frac{f}{g}$, where defined
Explanation. From $x_1 < x_2$ we get $f(x_1) + g(x_1) < f(x_2) + g(x_2)$. The product may fail ($f = g = x$ gives $x^2$), and so may the difference ($x - x = 0$).
am1-f-891 · level ipotesi · notes
The period of a periodic function is the smallest $T > 0$ with $f(x + T) = f(x)$ for every $x$. What happens for a constant function $f(x) = 5$?
  1. it has period $T = 1$
  2. it has period $T = 0$
  3. it is not periodic
  4. it has no smallest period
Explanation. The constant satisfies the condition for every $T > 0$, but the set $(0, +\infty)$ has no minimum: it is periodic without a smallest period.
am1-f-892 · level facile · notes
What is the period of the function $f(x) = \cos\left(\frac{x}{3}\right)$?
  1. $\frac{2\pi}{3}$
  2. $6\pi$
  3. $3\pi$
  4. $2\pi$
Explanation. $\cos(\omega x)$ has period $\frac{2\pi}{\omega}$; with $\omega = \frac13$ one gets $6\pi$.

4. Power functions (16)

am1-f-041 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of the power function $f(x) = x^{-2/3}$?
  1. $(0, \ip)$
  2. $\R \setminus \{0\}$
  3. $[0, \ip)$
  4. $\R$
Explanation. With $\alpha = \frac mn = -\frac23$: $n = 3$ is odd and $\alpha < 0$, so $D = \R \setminus \{0\}$ (the cube root is defined also for $x < 0$).
am1-f-042 · level facile · notes
$y$ is inversely proportional to $x$ and $y = 6$ for $x = 2$. What is $y$ for $x = 3$?
  1. $4$
  2. $9$
  3. $3$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Inverse proportionality: $y = \frac kx$ with $k = 6 \cdot 2 = 12$, hence $y = \frac{12}{3} = 4$. $9$ would come from direct proportionality.
am1-f-209 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of the power function $f(x) = x^{3/4}$?
  1. $\R$
  2. $[0, \ip)$
  3. $(0, \ip)$
  4. $\R \setminus \{0\}$
Explanation. $x^{3/4} = \sqrt[4]{x^3}$: the denominator $4$ of the exponent is even and the exponent is positive, so $D = [0, \ip)$.
am1-f-210 · level facile · notes
For a real exponent $\alpha$, the power function $f(x) = x^\alpha$ on $(0, \ip)$ is decreasing when:
  1. $\alpha > 1$
  2. $0 < \alpha < 1$
  3. $\alpha$ is an even integer
  4. $\alpha < 0$
Explanation. On $(0, \ip)$, $x^\alpha$ is increasing for $\alpha > 0$, constant for $\alpha = 0$ and decreasing for $\alpha < 0$ (for example $x^{-1} = \frac1x$).
am1-f-211 · level facile · notes
How many real roots can a polynomial of degree $5$ with real coefficients have at most, and how many at least?
  1. at most $5$, at least $1$
  2. at most $5$, at least $0$
  3. exactly $5$
  4. at most $4$, at least $1$
Explanation. A non-zero polynomial of degree $n$ has at most $n$ roots. If the degree is odd, the polynomial tends to $\pm\infty$ with opposite signs and by the intermediate zero theorem has at least one real root.
am1-f-212 · level facile · notes
$y$ is directly proportional to the square of $x$ ($y = k\,x^2$) and equals $y = 12$ for $x = 2$. What is $y$ for $x = 3$?
  1. $18$
  2. $36$
  3. $27$
  4. $8$
Explanation. $k = \frac{12}{4} = 3$, so $y = 3 \cdot 9 = 27$. $18$ would follow from direct proportionality to $x$.
am1-f-213 · level facile · notes
The power function $f(x) = x^{-1/3}$, defined on $\R \setminus \{0\}$, is:
  1. even
  2. neither even nor odd
  3. increasing on $\R \setminus \{0\}$
  4. odd
Explanation. $(-x)^{-1/3} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{-x}} = -\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{x}}$: it is odd. It is decreasing on $(-\infty, 0)$ and on $(0, \ip)$ separately.
am1-f-214 · level facile · notes
For $x \in (0, 1)$, how are the values $x^2$, $x^{1/2}$ and $x$ ordered?
  1. $x^{1/2} < x < x^2$
  2. $x^2 < x < x^{1/2}$
  3. $x < x^2 < x^{1/2}$
  4. $x^2 < x^{1/2} < x$
Explanation. For a base in $(0,1)$ the power decreases as the exponent increases: $x^2 < x^1 < x^{1/2}$. For example with $x = \frac14$: $\frac1{16} < \frac14 < \frac12$.
am1-f-496 · level facile · notes
By Boyle's law, at constant temperature the pressure $p$ and the volume $V$ of a gas are inversely proportional ($p\,V$ constant). If $V$ triples, $p$:
  1. drops to one third
  2. triples
  3. decreases by $3$ units
  4. drops to one ninth
Explanation. From $p\,V = k$ we get $p' = \frac{k}{3V} = \frac{p}{3}$. Dropping to one ninth would be the case $p\,V^2$ constant.
am1-f-497 · level facile · notes
Which function expresses a direct proportionality between $x$ and $y$ (i.e. $\frac{y}{x}$ constant for $x \neq 0$)?
  1. $y = 5x + 1$
  2. $y = \frac{5}{x}$
  3. $y = 5x$
  4. $y = 5x^2$
Explanation. In direct proportionality $y = k\,x$. With $5x + 1$ the ratio is not constant, $\frac5x$ is an inverse proportionality, $5x^2$ is proportional to the square.
am1-f-498 · level facile · notes
What are the images of the power functions $f(x) = x^{4}$ and $g(x) = x^{5}$, defined on $\R$?
  1. $\R$ and $\R$
  2. $[0, \ip)$ and $[0, \ip)$
  3. $\R$ and $[0, \ip)$
  4. $[0, \ip)$ and $\R$
Explanation. With an even integer exponent the power is $\ge 0$ and takes every value $\ge 0$; with an odd exponent it is a bijection of $\R$ onto itself.
am1-f-499 · level facile · notes
How many real solutions do the equations $x^{6} = -2$ and $x^{7} = -2$ have?
  1. none and one
  2. two and one
  3. none and none
  4. one and one
Explanation. A power with even exponent is $\ge 0$, so $x^6 = -2$ has no solutions; $x^7$ is a bijection of $\R$, so $x^7 = -2$ has the unique solution $x = -\sqrt[7]{2}$.
am1-f-893 · level ipotesi · notes
For a non-rational real $\alpha$ (e.g. $\alpha = \sqrt2$) the power function $x^\alpha$ is defined only for $x > 0$ (or $x \ge 0$). Why does it not extend to negatives like $x^{1/3}$?
  1. because $\sqrt2 > 1$
  2. because $x^{\sqrt2}$ would always be negative
  3. because $a^b$ with a negative base makes sense only for integer $b$ or a reduced rational $\frac nm$ with odd $m$
  4. because for $x < 0$ we would have $x^{\sqrt2} = 0$
Explanation. $x^{1/3}$ extends via $\sqrt[3]{x} = -\sqrt[3]{-x}$ because the denominator $3$ is odd; an irrational exponent is not of that kind.
am1-f-894 · level facile · notes
A polynomial of degree $n$ is $p(x) = a_n x^n + \dots + a_1 x + a_0$ with $a_n \neq 0$. What is the degree of $p(x) = (x^2 + 1)(2x^3 - x) - 2x^5$?
  1. $5$
  2. $6$
  3. $3$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $(x^2+1)(2x^3 - x) = 2x^5 + x^3 - x$; removing $2x^5$ leaves $x^3 - x$, of degree $3$: the top-degree terms may cancel.
am1-f-895 · level facile · notes
If $x = 2$ is a root of the polynomial $p(x) = x^3 - 3x^2 + 4$, which factorisation is correct?
  1. $(x - 2)^2\,(x + 1)$
  2. $(x - 2)(x^2 + 1)$
  3. $(x + 2)(x - 1)^2$
  4. $(x - 2)(x + 2)(x - 1)$
Explanation. Dividing by $x - 2$ gives $x^2 - x - 2 = (x - 2)(x + 1)$: $2$ is a double root. Check: $(x-2)^2(x+1) = (x^2 - 4x + 4)(x + 1) = x^3 - 3x^2 + 4$.
am1-f-896 · level facile · notes
Power functions $x^n$ with positive integer $n$: for which $n$ is $x^n$ (strictly) increasing on all of $\R$?
  1. for every $n \ge 1$
  2. for even $n$
  3. only for $n = 1$
  4. for odd $n$
Explanation. For odd $n$, $x^n$ is odd and increasing on $\R$; for even $n$ it is even, decreasing on $(-\infty, 0]$ and increasing on $[0, +\infty)$.

5. Exponential and logarithmic functions (15)

am1-f-043 · level facile · notes
Change of base: for $a, b > 0$ different from $1$ and $x > 0$, $\log_a x$ equals:
  1. $\frac{\log_b x}{\log_b a}$
  2. $\frac{\log_b a}{\log_b x}$
  3. $\log_b x \cdot \log_b a$
  4. $\log_b \frac{x}{a}$
Explanation. From $x = a^{\log_a x}$, applying $\log_b$: $\log_b x = \log_a x \cdot \log_b a$.
am1-f-044 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = \log_{1/2} x$ is:
  1. increasing in $(0, \ip)$
  2. decreasing in $\R$
  3. increasing in $\R$
  4. decreasing in $(0, \ip)$
Explanation. Logarithms are defined on $(0,\ip)$; with base $a \in (0,1)$ they are decreasing.
am1-f-215 · level facile · notes
What is the solution set of the inequality $\left(\frac12\right)^x > 4$?
  1. $(2, \ip)$
  2. $(-2, \ip)$
  3. $(-\infty, -2)$
  4. $(-\infty, 2)$
Explanation. $4 = \left(\frac12\right)^{-2}$ and the exponential with base $\frac12 < 1$ is decreasing: $\left(\frac12\right)^x > \left(\frac12\right)^{-2} \iff x < -2$.
am1-f-216 · level facile · notes
What is the solution of the equation $\log(2x - 1) = 0$ ($\log$ is the natural logarithm)?
  1. $x = 1$
  2. $x = \frac12$
  3. $x = \frac{e + 1}{2}$
  4. no solution
Explanation. $\log y = 0 \iff y = 1$: $2x - 1 = 1$, i.e. $x = 1$ (which satisfies the condition $2x - 1 > 0$). $\frac{e+1}{2}$ solves $\log(2x-1) = 1$.
am1-f-217 · level facile · notes
What is the image of the exponential function $f(x) = a^x$ with base $a > 0$, $a \neq 1$?
  1. $\R$
  2. $(0, \ip)$
  3. $[0, \ip)$
  4. $[1, \ip)$
Explanation. For every admissible base $a^x > 0$ and each $y > 0$ is attained at $x = \log_a y$. The value $0$ is never reached (horizontal asymptote $y = 0$).
am1-f-218 · level facile · notes
What is $e^{2 \log 3}$ ($\log$ is the natural logarithm)?
  1. $6$
  2. $3$
  3. $2\,e^3$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $e^{2\log 3} = \left(e^{\log 3}\right)^2 = 3^2 = 9$, since exponential and natural logarithm are inverse functions.
am1-f-219 · level facile · notes
Using the change of base formula, what is $\log_4 8$?
  1. $2$
  2. $\frac23$
  3. $\frac32$
  4. $\frac12$
Explanation. $\log_4 8 = \frac{\log_2 8}{\log_2 4} = \frac32$; indeed $4^{3/2} = 8$.
am1-f-220 · level facile · notes
What is the solution set of the inequality $\log_2 (x - 1) < 3$?
  1. $(-\infty, 9)$
  2. $(1, 9)$
  3. $(1, 7)$
  4. $(9, \ip)$
Explanation. We need $x - 1 > 0$; since $\log_2$ is increasing, $\log_2(x-1) < 3 \iff x - 1 < 8$. Hence $1 < x < 9$.
am1-f-500 · level facile · notes
For which base $a$ is the exponential function $f(x) = a^x$ decreasing on $\R$?
  1. $a = \frac32$
  2. $a = \frac23$
  3. $a = e$
  4. $a = 1$
Explanation. $a^x$ is increasing for $a > 1$, decreasing for $0 < a < 1$ and constant for $a = 1$.
am1-f-501 · level facile · notes
What are the real solutions of the equation $e^{2x} - 3e^{x} + 2 = 0$ ($\log$ is the natural logarithm)?
  1. $x = 1$ and $x = 2$
  2. $x = 0$ and $x = 2$
  3. $x = 0$ and $x = \log 2$
  4. only $x = \log 2$
Explanation. With $t = e^x > 0$ we get $t^2 - 3t + 2 = 0$, i.e. $t = 1$ or $t = 2$; hence $x = 0$ or $x = \log 2$. $1$ and $2$ are the values of $t$, not of $x$.
am1-f-502 · level facile · notes
Given $\log 2 \approx 0.693$ and $\log 10 \approx 2.303$ (natural $\log$), what is $\log_{2} 10$ approximately?
  1. $\approx 0.30$
  2. $\approx 1.60$
  3. $\approx 5$
  4. $\approx 3.32$
Explanation. Change of base: $\log_2 10 = \frac{\log 10}{\log 2} \approx \frac{2.303}{0.693} \approx 3.32$. The value $0.30$ is $\log_{10} 2$, the reciprocal.
am1-f-503 · level facile · notes
For which $x$ is $\log_{1/3} x > 0$?
  1. $x > 1$
  2. $0 < x < 1$
  3. $x > \frac13$
  4. $0 < x < \frac13$
Explanation. With base $\frac13 < 1$ the logarithm is decreasing and equals $0$ at $x = 1$: it is positive for $0 < x < 1$.
am1-f-897 · level ipotesi · notes
Change-of-base formula $\log_a x = \frac{\log_b x}{\log_b a}$: which conditions are needed on the new base $b$?
  1. none, $b$ is any real number
  2. $b > 1$
  3. $b > 0$ and $b \neq 1$
  4. $b = e$ or $b = 10$
Explanation. $\log_b$ requires a positive base different from $1$; the denominator $\log_b a$ is non-zero because $a \neq 1$. $0 < b < 1$ is fine too.
am1-f-898 · level teorema · notes
Exponential functions $f(x) = a^x$ with $a > 0$, $a \neq 1$: which property holds?
  1. monotone according to $a$; image $(0, +\infty)$
  2. they are increasing for every $a$; the image is $\R$
  3. they are even; the image is $[1, +\infty)$
  4. they are increasing if $a > 1$; the image is $[0, +\infty)$
Explanation. $a^x > 0$ for every $x$ and every positive value is taken; monotonicity depends on the base. $0$ is not a value.
am1-f-899 · level teorema · notes
The logarithm function $\log_a: (0, +\infty) \rr \R$ ($a > 0$, $a \neq 1$) is the inverse of which function, and which identities hold?
  1. of $x^a$; $\log_a(x^a) = x$
  2. of $a^x$; both compositions are the identity
  3. of $\frac1{a^x}$; $\log_a x \cdot a^x = 1$
  4. of $a^x$; $\log_a(a^x) = x$ only for $x > 0$
Explanation. $a^x$ is a bijection from $\R$ to $(0, +\infty)$ and $\log_a$ is its inverse: the two compositions give the identity on their domains.

6. Trigonometric functions (21)

am1-f-045 · level facile · notes
Double-angle formula: $\cos(2x)$ equals:
  1. $2 \sin x \cos x$
  2. $\cos^2 x - \sin^2 x$
  3. $2\cos^2 x + 1$
  4. $\cos^2 x + \sin^2 x$
Explanation. $\cos(2x) = \cos^2 x - \sin^2 x = 2\cos^2 x - 1$. $2\sin x\cos x$ is $\sin(2x)$.
am1-f-046 · level facile · notes
What is $\cos\frac{2\pi}{3}$?
  1. $-\frac12$
  2. $\frac12$
  3. $-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
  4. $\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
Explanation. $\frac{2\pi}{3} = \pi - \frac\pi3$, hence $\cos\frac{2\pi}{3} = -\cos\frac\pi3 = -\frac12$.
am1-f-221 · level facile · notes
What is $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4}$?
  1. $-1$
  2. $\frac{\sqrt2}{2}$
  3. $\sqrt3$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\tan$ has period $\pi$: $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4} = \tan\frac{\pi}{4} = 1$ (sine and cosine are both $-\frac{\sqrt2}{2}$).
am1-f-222 · level facile · notes
Addition formula: $\sin(\alpha + \beta)$ equals:
  1. $\sin\alpha\cos\beta + \cos\alpha\sin\beta$
  2. $\sin\alpha + \sin\beta$
  3. $\sin\alpha\sin\beta + \cos\alpha\cos\beta$
  4. $\sin\alpha\cos\beta - \cos\alpha\sin\beta$
Explanation. This is the sine addition formula. $\cos\alpha\cos\beta + \sin\alpha\sin\beta$ is $\cos(\alpha - \beta)$, and the minus sign gives $\sin(\alpha - \beta)$.
am1-f-223 · level facile · notes
What are the solutions of the equation $\sin x = \frac12$ in the interval $[0, 2\pi)$?
  1. $x = \frac\pi6$
  2. $x = \frac\pi6$ and $x = \frac{5\pi}{6}$
  3. $x = \frac\pi3$ and $x = \frac{2\pi}{3}$
  4. $x = \frac\pi6$ and $x = \frac{7\pi}{6}$
Explanation. $\sin\frac\pi6 = \frac12$ and, since $\sin(\pi - x) = \sin x$, also $\sin\frac{5\pi}{6} = \frac12$. At $\frac{7\pi}6$ the sine equals $-\frac12$.
am1-f-224 · level facile · notes
If $\cos x = \frac35$ and $x \in \left(-\frac\pi2, 0\right)$, what is $\sin x$?
  1. $\frac45$
  2. $\frac25$
  3. $-\frac45$
  4. $-\frac35$
Explanation. $\sin^2 x = 1 - \frac{9}{25} = \frac{16}{25}$, and in the fourth quadrant the sine is negative: $\sin x = -\frac45$.
am1-f-225 · level facile · notes
A harmonic oscillation is described by $y(t) = 3 \sin(4t + 1)$. What are its amplitude and period?
  1. amplitude $3$, period $4$
  2. amplitude $3$, period $\frac\pi2$
  3. amplitude $4$, period $\frac{2\pi}{3}$
  4. amplitude $3$, period $8\pi$
Explanation. In $A\sin(\omega t + \varphi)$ the amplitude is $|A| = 3$ and the period is $\frac{2\pi}{\omega} = \frac{2\pi}{4} = \frac\pi2$; the phase $1$ does not change the period.
am1-f-226 · level facile · notes
Which identity holds for every $x$ where both sides are defined?
  1. $1 + \tan^2 x = \frac{1}{\sin^2 x}$
  2. $\sin(2x) = 2\sin x$
  3. $\cos(x + \pi) = \cos x$
  4. $1 + \tan^2 x = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x}$
Explanation. Dividing $\sin^2 x + \cos^2 x = 1$ by $\cos^2 x$ gives $\tan^2 x + 1 = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x}$. Instead $\sin 2x = 2\sin x\cos x$ and $\cos(x + \pi) = -\cos x$.
am1-f-504 · level facile · notes
On the unit circle (centre the origin, radius $1$), the point associated with the angle $x$ (in radians, measured counterclockwise from the positive $x$-axis) has coordinates:
  1. $(\cos x, \sin x)$
  2. $(\sin x, \cos x)$
  3. $(\cos x, \tan x)$
  4. $(x, \sin x)$
Explanation. By definition the cosine is the abscissa and the sine the ordinate of the point of the unit circle determined by the angle $x$.
am1-f-505 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of the function $\tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x}$?
  1. $\R \setminus \{k\pi : k \in \Z\}$
  2. $\R \setminus \left\{\frac\pi2 + 2k\pi : k \in \Z\right\}$
  3. $\R$
  4. $\R \setminus \left\{\frac\pi2 + k\pi : k \in \Z\right\}$
Explanation. The zeros of the cosine, i.e. $\frac\pi2 + k\pi$, must be excluded. The zeros of the sine $k\pi$ are excluded for the cotangent.
am1-f-506 · level facile · notes
A harmonic oscillation $y(t) = A \sin(\omega t + \varphi)$, with $A > 0$ and $\omega > 0$, has period $T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega}$ and frequency $\frac1T$. If $T = 0.5$ seconds, what are the frequency and $\omega$?
  1. frequency $0.5$, $\omega = \pi$
  2. frequency $2$, $\omega = 2$
  3. frequency $2$, $\omega = 4\pi$
  4. frequency $2$, $\omega = \pi$
Explanation. The frequency is $\frac{1}{0.5} = 2$ oscillations per second and $\omega = \frac{2\pi}{T} = 4\pi$.
am1-f-507 · level facile · notes
Using the double-angle formula $\sin(2x) = 2\sin x\cos x$, what is $\sin\frac{\pi}{12}\,\cos\frac{\pi}{12}$?
  1. $\frac12$
  2. $\frac14$
  3. $\frac{\sqrt3}{4}$
  4. $\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
Explanation. $\sin\frac{\pi}{12}\cos\frac{\pi}{12} = \frac12 \sin\frac{\pi}{6} = \frac12 \cdot \frac12 = \frac14$.
am1-f-900 · level facile · notes
Sine and cosine of $x$ are the coordinates of the point $P$ on the unit circle (radius $1$) at angle $x$. Which identity follows directly from the definition?
  1. $\sin x + \cos x = 1$
  2. $\sin x = \cos(-x)$
  3. $\sin^2 x + \cos^2 x = 1$
  4. $\sin x \cdot \cos x = 1$
Explanation. $P = (\cos x, \sin x)$ lies on the circle $X^2 + Y^2 = 1$. For $x = \frac\pi4$ the first option would give $\sqrt2 = 1$.
am1-f-901 · level ipotesi · notes
If $\sin x = \sin y$, can one conclude that $x = y$?
  1. no, $\sin$ is not injective
  2. yes, because $\sin$ is a function
  3. yes, if $x, y \in [0, 2\pi]$
  4. no, but it holds if $x, y \in [0, \pi]$
Explanation. $\sin$ is periodic and not injective; even on $[0, \pi]$ we have $\sin\frac\pi6 = \sin\frac{5\pi}{6}$. It is injective e.g. on $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$.
am1-f-902 · level facile · notes
What is $\sin\left(-\frac{7\pi}{6}\right)$?
  1. $-\frac12$
  2. $-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
  3. $\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$
  4. $\frac12$
Explanation. $\sin$ is odd: $\sin\left(-\frac{7\pi}{6}\right) = -\sin\frac{7\pi}{6} = -\left(-\frac12\right) = \frac12$.
am1-f-903 · level teorema · notes
Superposition of vibrations with the same angular frequency: $a\sin(\omega t) + b\cos(\omega t) = A\sin(\omega t + \varphi)$. What is the amplitude $A$?
  1. $a + b$
  2. $\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}$
  3. $\max(a, b)$
  4. $a^2 + b^2$
Explanation. With $a = A\cos\varphi$ and $b = A\sin\varphi$ we get $a^2 + b^2 = A^2$. For $a = 3$, $b = 4$: amplitude $5$, not $7$.
am1-f-904 · level facile · notes
For the elementary vibration $t \mapsto 5\cos(4t)$, what are the period $T$ and the frequency $\nu = \frac{\omega}{2\pi}$?
  1. $T = 4$, $\nu = \frac14$
  2. $T = 8\pi$, $\nu = \frac{1}{8\pi}$
  3. $T = \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\nu = \frac{2}{\pi}$
  4. $T = \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\nu = 4$
Explanation. $\omega = 4$: $T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega} = \frac\pi2$ and $\nu = \frac{4}{2\pi} = \frac{2}{\pi}$, i.e. $\frac1T$. The amplitude $5$ does not affect the period.
am1-f-905 · level facile · notes
The function $h(t) = \sin(2t) + \sqrt3\cos(2t)$ is written as $A\sin(2t + \varphi)$. What are $A$ and $\varphi$?
  1. $A = 2$, $\varphi = \frac\pi3$
  2. $A = 1 + \sqrt3$, $\varphi = 0$
  3. $A = 2$, $\varphi = \frac\pi6$
  4. $A = 4$, $\varphi = \frac\pi3$
Explanation. $A = \sqrt{1 + 3} = 2$, $\cos\varphi = \frac12$, $\sin\varphi = \frac{\sqrt3}{2}$, so $\varphi = \frac\pi3$. With $\frac\pi6$ sine and cosine are swapped.
am1-f-906 · level facile · notes
The function $h(t) = t\sin(\omega t)$, for $t \ge 0$, describes an amplified vibration. Between which curves does its graph lie?
  1. between $y = -1$ and $y = 1$
  2. between $y = 0$ and $y = t$
  3. between $y = -t^2$ and $y = t^2$
  4. between the lines $y = -t$ and $y = t$
Explanation. From $-1 \le \sin(\omega t) \le 1$, multiplying by $t \ge 0$: $-t \le h(t) \le t$; the oscillations grow linearly.
am1-f-907 · level ipotesi · notes
The vibration $h(t) = e^{-kt}\sin t$, $t \ge 0$, is damped for $k > 0$. What happens for $k < 0$?
  1. it stays damped, more slowly
  2. it becomes amplified: the amplitude $e^{-kt} = e^{|k|t}$ grows
  3. it becomes an elementary vibration with constant amplitude
  4. it is no longer periodic or oscillating
Explanation. The graph lies between $\pm e^{-kt}$: for $k > 0$ the envelope tends to $0$, for $k < 0$ it grows exponentially, for $k = 0$ one gets the elementary vibration $\sin t$.
am1-f-908 · level facile · notes
At which instants $t \ge 0$ does the damped vibration $h(t) = e^{-t}\sin t$ vanish?
  1. $t = k\pi$, $k \in \N$
  2. never, since $e^{-t} > 0$
  3. $t = \frac\pi2 + k\pi$, $k \in \N$
  4. only at $t = 0$
Explanation. $e^{-t}$ never vanishes, so $h(t) = 0$ if and only if $\sin t = 0$: damping changes the amplitude, not the zeros.

7. Integer part and fractional part functions (14)

am1-f-047 · level facile · notes
The fractional part function $(x) = x - [x]$, where $[x]$ is the integer part (the largest integer $\le x$), takes values in:
  1. $[0, 1)$
  2. $(0, 1]$
  3. $[0, 1]$
  4. $(-1, 1)$
Explanation. From $[x] \le x < [x] + 1$ we get $0 \le x - [x] < 1$; it is $0$ exactly at the integers.
am1-f-048 · level facile · notes
As a piecewise-defined function, $|x - 2|$ equals:
  1. $x - 2$ for $x < 2$ and $2 - x$ for $x \ge 2$
  2. $x + 2$ for $x < -2$ and $x - 2$ for $x \ge -2$
  3. $2 - x$ for $x < 2$ and $x - 2$ for $x \ge 2$
  4. $x - 2$ for every $x$
Explanation. For $x \ge 2$ the argument is $\ge 0$ and stays unchanged; for $x < 2$ it is negative and changes sign: $2 - x$.
am1-f-227 · level facile · notes
With $[x]$ the integer part ($[x]$ is the integer $n$ with $n \le x < n+1$), what is $[-2.5] + [2.5]$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $-1$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. $[2.5] = 2$ and $[-2.5] = -3$ (not $-2$: we need $n \le -2.5$). The sum is $-1$.
am1-f-228 · level facile · notes
With $[x]$ the integer part ($n \le x < n+1$), what is the solution set of the equation $[x] = 3$?
  1. $[3, 4)$
  2. $\{3\}$
  3. $(3, 4]$
  4. $[2, 3)$
Explanation. $[x] = 3 \iff 3 \le x < 4$: the integer part is piecewise constant, with jumps at the integers.
am1-f-229 · level facile · notes
Is the fractional part function $(x) = x - [x]$, with $[x]$ the integer part, periodic? With which minimal period?
  1. it is not periodic
  2. yes, period $2$
  3. yes, period $\frac12$
  4. yes, period $1$
Explanation. $[x + 1] = [x] + 1$, so $(x + 1) = (x)$; no $T \in (0, 1)$ works since $(0) = 0$ and $(T) = T \neq 0$.
am1-f-230 · level facile · notes
What is the solution set of the inequality $|2x - 3| \ge 5$?
  1. $[-1, 4]$
  2. $(-\infty, -1] \cup [4, \ip)$
  3. $[4, \ip)$
  4. $(-\infty, -4] \cup [1, \ip)$
Explanation. $|2x - 3| \ge 5 \iff 2x - 3 \le -5$ or $2x - 3 \ge 5$, i.e. $x \le -1$ or $x \ge 4$.
am1-f-231 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^2$ for $x \le 1$ and $f(x) = 3 - x$ for $x > 1$. What is $f(-2) + f(4)$?
  1. $5$
  2. $-3$
  3. $3$
  4. $15$
Explanation. $-2 \le 1$, so $f(-2) = 4$; $4 > 1$, so $f(4) = 3 - 4 = -1$. The sum is $3$.
am1-f-232 · level facile · notes
For which $x \in \R$ does the equality $|x - 1| = 1 - x$ hold?
  1. $x \le 1$
  2. $x \ge 1$
  3. only $x = 1$
  4. for every $x \in \R$
Explanation. $|a| = -a$ exactly when $a \le 0$: here $a = x - 1 \le 0$, i.e. $x \le 1$.
am1-f-508 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = 2x + 1$ for $x < 0$ and $f(x) = 1 - x^2$ for $x \ge 0$. What is the solution set of $f(x) = 0$?
  1. $\left\{-\frac12, 1\right\}$
  2. $\left\{-\frac12\right\}$
  3. $\{-1, 1\}$
  4. $\left\{-\frac12, -1, 1\right\}$
Explanation. For $x < 0$: $2x + 1 = 0$ gives $x = -\frac12$, admissible. For $x \ge 0$: $1 - x^2 = 0$ gives $x = 1$ ($x = -1$ is discarded, since it is not $\ge 0$).
am1-f-509 · level facile · notes
What is the piecewise expression of the function $f(x) = x + |x|$?
  1. $2x$ for $x < 0$ and $0$ for $x \ge 0$
  2. $0$ for every $x$
  3. $2x$ for every $x$
  4. $0$ for $x < 0$ and $2x$ for $x \ge 0$
Explanation. For $x < 0$ we have $|x| = -x$ and $f(x) = 0$; for $x \ge 0$ we have $|x| = x$ and $f(x) = 2x$.
am1-f-510 · level facile · notes
With $[x]$ the integer part (the largest integer $\le x$), what is the solution set of the equation $[x]^2 = 4$?
  1. $\{-2, 2\}$
  2. $[2, 3)$
  3. $[-2, -1) \cup [2, 3)$
  4. $(-3, -2] \cup [2, 3)$
Explanation. $[x]^2 = 4$ means $[x] = 2$ or $[x] = -2$, i.e. $2 \le x < 3$ or $-2 \le x < -1$.
am1-f-511 · level facile · notes
For which $x \in \R$ does $|x + 1| + |x - 1| = 2$ hold?
  1. $\{-1, 1\}$
  2. $[-1, 1]$
  3. $\{0\}$
  4. $\R$
Explanation. $|x + 1| + |x - 1|$ is the sum of the distances of $x$ from $-1$ and from $1$: it equals $2$ for $x \in [-1, 1]$ and is larger than $2$ outside this interval.
am1-f-910 · level facile · notes
The ceiling function $\lceil x \rceil$ maps $x$ to the smallest integer $\ge x$. Which set of $x$ has $\lceil x \rceil = 3$?
  1. $(2, 3]$
  2. $[3, 4)$
  3. $[2, 3)$
  4. $(3, 4]$
Explanation. $\lceil x \rceil = n \Leftrightarrow n - 1 < x \le n$: for $n = 3$ one gets $(2, 3]$. $[3, 4)$ is the set where the floor $[x]$ equals $3$.
am1-f-911 · level ipotesi · notes
The piecewise function $f(x) = x + 1$ for $x < 0$ and $f(x) = a$ for $x \ge 0$ has a jump at $0$, unless:
  1. $a = 0$
  2. $a = -1$
  3. $a = 1$, the value approached by $x + 1$ as $x \rr 0$
  4. $a$ is any integer
Explanation. The first piece approaches $0 + 1 = 1$ as $x \rr 0^-$: the two pieces join only if $f(0) = a = 1$.

8. Hyperbolic functions (12)

am1-f-049 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, which identity holds for every $x \in \R$?
  1. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x = 1$
  2. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = -1$
  3. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = \cosH (2x)$
  4. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = 1$
Explanation. With $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}2$ and $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}2$: $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x = \frac{4}{4} = 1$ (hyperbola $X^2 - Y^2 = 1$).
am1-f-050 · level facile · notes
Hyperbolic sine $\sinH x$ and hyperbolic cosine $\cosH x$: which statement is correct?
  1. $\sinH$ is odd and $\cosH$ is even
  2. both are even
  3. $\sinH$ is even and $\cosH$ is odd
  4. both are odd
Explanation. Replacing $x$ with $-x$ swaps $e^x$ and $e^{-x}$: $\sinH(-x) = -\sinH x$ and $\cosH(-x) = \cosH x$.
am1-f-233 · level facile · notes
What is $\cosH(\log 2)$, with $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\log$ the natural logarithm?
  1. $\frac32$
  2. $\frac54$
  3. $\frac34$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $e^{\log 2} = 2$ and $e^{-\log 2} = \frac12$: $\cosH(\log 2) = \frac{2 + \frac12}{2} = \frac54$.
am1-f-234 · level facile · notes
What is the image of the hyperbolic cosine $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, $x \in \R$?
  1. $\R$
  2. $[-1, 1]$
  3. $(0, \ip)$
  4. $[1, \ip)$
Explanation. By the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality $\frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2} \ge \sqrt{e^x e^{-x}} = 1$, with equality at $x = 0$; and $\cosH x \rr \ip$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$.
am1-f-235 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, what is $\cosH x + \sinH x$?
  1. $e^x$
  2. $e^{-x}$
  3. $1$
  4. $2\,e^x$
Explanation. Adding the two definitions the $e^{-x}$ terms cancel: $\frac{2e^x}{2} = e^x$. Instead $\cosH x - \sinH x = e^{-x}$.
am1-f-236 · level facile · notes
Duplication formula for the hyperbolic sine $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$: $\sinH(2x)$ equals:
  1. $2\sinH x$
  2. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x$
  3. $2\sinH x\,\cosH x$
  4. $\sinH^2 x + \cosH^2 x$
Explanation. $2\sinH x\cosH x = 2\,\frac{(e^x - e^{-x})(e^x + e^{-x})}{4} = \frac{e^{2x} - e^{-2x}}{2} = \sinH(2x)$. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x$ is $\cosH(2x)$.
am1-f-237 · level facile · notes
The hyperbolic tangent $\tanH x = \frac{\sinH x}{\cosH x}$, $x \in \R$, has image:
  1. $\R$
  2. $(-1, 1)$
  3. $[-1, 1]$
  4. $(0, 1)$
Explanation. $\tanH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{e^x + e^{-x}}$ is increasing, odd, and tends to $\pm 1$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$ without attaining these values.
am1-f-238 · level facile · notes
For which $x \in \R$ is $\sinH x = 0$, with $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$?
  1. for $x = k\pi$, $k \in \Z$
  2. for no $x$
  3. for $x = 0$ and $x = \log 2$
  4. only for $x = 0$
Explanation. $e^x - e^{-x} = 0 \iff e^{2x} = 1 \iff x = 0$. Unlike the sine, the hyperbolic sine is strictly increasing and has a single zero.
am1-f-512 · level facile · notes
The hyperbolic sine $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$, $x \in \R$, is:
  1. increasing on $\R$, with image $(-1, 1)$
  2. decreasing on $(-\infty, 0]$ and increasing on $[0, \ip)$
  3. periodic with period $2\pi$
  4. increasing on $\R$, with image $\R$
Explanation. $e^x$ is increasing and $e^{-x}$ decreasing, so their difference is increasing; moreover $\sinH x \rr \pm\infty$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$. The image $(-1, 1)$ is that of the hyperbolic tangent.
am1-f-513 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, what is $\cosH(2x)$ equal to?
  1. $\cosH^2 x - \sinH^2 x$
  2. $2\cosH x$
  3. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x$
  4. $1 - 2\sinH^2 x$
Explanation. $\cosH^2 x + \sinH^2 x = \frac{(e^{2x} + 2 + e^{-2x}) + (e^{2x} - 2 + e^{-2x})}{4} = \frac{e^{2x} + e^{-2x}}{2}$. The difference equals $1$; $1 - 2\sinH^2 x$ wrongly copies the cosine formula.
am1-f-514 · level facile · notes
What is $\sinH(\log 3)$, with $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\log$ the natural logarithm?
  1. $\frac53$
  2. $\frac43$
  3. $1$
  4. $\frac83$
Explanation. $e^{\log 3} = 3$ and $e^{-\log 3} = \frac13$, so $\sinH(\log 3) = \frac{3 - \frac13}{2} = \frac43$. $\frac53$ is $\cosH(\log 3)$, $\frac83$ forgets the factor $\frac12$.
am1-f-515 · level facile · notes
With $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ and $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$, what is the addition formula for $\sinH(x + y)$?
  1. $\sinH x \cosH y + \cosH x \sinH y$
  2. $\sinH x \cosH y - \cosH x \sinH y$
  3. $\cosH x \cosH y + \sinH x \sinH y$
  4. $\sinH x + \sinH y$
Explanation. Expanding with exponentials gives the same form as the sine formula: $\sinH(x + y) = \sinH x \cosH y + \cosH x \sinH y$. The third expression is $\cosH(x + y)$.

9. Operations on graphs (18)

am1-f-051 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(x + 3)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a translation to the right by $3$
  2. a translation to the left by $3$
  3. an upward translation by $3$
  4. a downward translation by $3$
Explanation. The value taken by $f$ at $x_0$ is now taken at $x_0 - 3$: the graph moves to the left. Moving it to the right is the typical mistake.
am1-f-052 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(|x|)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. reflecting across the $x$-axis the parts where $f(x) < 0$
  2. reflecting the whole graph across the $y$-axis
  3. keeping the part with $x \ge 0$ and reflecting it across the $y$-axis
  4. keeping the part with $x \le 0$ and reflecting it across the $x$-axis
Explanation. $f(|x|)$ is even and coincides with $f$ for $x \ge 0$. Reflecting the negative parts across the $x$-axis gives $|f(x)|$ instead.
am1-f-239 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = -f(x)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a reflection in the $y$-axis
  2. a reflection in the origin
  3. a reflection in the $x$-axis
  4. a downward translation
Explanation. The point $(x, f(x))$ becomes $(x, -f(x))$: the ordinate changes sign, i.e. the graph is reflected in the $x$-axis.
am1-f-240 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(2x)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a horizontal compression by a factor $2$ (abscissas are halved)
  2. a horizontal stretch by a factor $2$ (abscissas are doubled)
  3. a vertical stretch by a factor $2$
  4. a translation to the left by $2$
Explanation. The value taken by $f$ at $x_0$ is now taken at $\frac{x_0}{2}$: the graph is compressed horizontally toward the $y$-axis.
am1-f-241 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = |f(x)|$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$:
  1. by keeping the part with $x \ge 0$ and reflecting it in the $y$-axis
  2. by reflecting the whole graph in the $x$-axis
  3. by deleting the parts with $f(x) < 0$
  4. by reflecting in the $x$-axis the parts with $f(x) < 0$
Explanation. Where $f(x) \ge 0$ the graph is unchanged, where $f(x) < 0$ the ordinate changes sign. The first option describes $f(|x|)$ instead.
am1-f-242 · level facile · notes
The parabola $y = x^2$ is translated $2$ to the right and $3$ down. What is the equation of the new curve?
  1. $y = (x + 2)^2 - 3$
  2. $y = (x - 2)^2 - 3$
  3. $y = (x - 2)^2 + 3$
  4. $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$
Explanation. Translating right by $2$ means replacing $x$ by $x - 2$; translating down by $3$ means subtracting $3$: vertex at $(2, -3)$.
am1-f-243 · level facile · notes
If the image of $f$ is $[-1, 2]$, what is the image of $g(x) = 3 f(x) + 1$?
  1. $[-3, 6]$
  2. $[0, 3]$
  3. $[-2, 7]$
  4. $[2, 5]$
Explanation. Vertical stretch by a factor $3$ and upward translation by $1$: the endpoints become $3(-1) + 1 = -2$ and $3 \cdot 2 + 1 = 7$.
am1-f-244 · level facile · notes
If $f$ has a unique zero at $x = 3$, where does $g(x) = f(x - 1)$ vanish?
  1. at $x = 4$
  2. at $x = 2$
  3. at $x = 3$
  4. at $x = -3$
Explanation. $g(x) = 0 \iff x - 1 = 3 \iff x = 4$: the graph of $f(x - 1)$ is that of $f$ translated $1$ to the right.
am1-f-516 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = f(x) - 4$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a translation upwards by $4$
  2. a translation downwards by $4$
  3. a translation to the right by $4$
  4. a translation to the left by $4$
Explanation. Every ordinate decreases by $4$, the abscissas stay the same: it is a vertical translation downwards.
am1-f-517 · level facile · notes
The function $f$ vanishes exactly at $x = -6$ and $x = 9$. Where does $g(x) = f(3x)$ vanish?
  1. at $x = -18$ and $x = 27$
  2. at $x = -3$ and $x = 12$
  3. at $x = -2$ and $x = 3$
  4. at $x = -6$ and $x = 9$
Explanation. $g(x) = 0$ if and only if $3x = -6$ or $3x = 9$: the graph is compressed horizontally and the abscissas are divided by $3$.
am1-f-518 · level facile · notes
The image of $f$ is the interval $[-3, 2]$. What is the image of $g(x) = |f(x)|$?
  1. $[2, 3]$
  2. $[0, 2]$
  3. $[-3, 3]$
  4. $[0, 3]$
Explanation. The values of $f$ in $[-3, 0]$ become $[0, 3]$, those in $[0, 2]$ stay; the union is $[0, 3]$.
am1-f-519 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = |\sin x|$ is obtained by reflecting in the $x$-axis the negative parts of $y = \sin x$. What is the (minimal) period of $|\sin x|$?
  1. $\pi$
  2. $2\pi$
  3. $\frac\pi2$
  4. $4\pi$
Explanation. $|\sin(x + \pi)| = |-\sin x| = |\sin x|$, and no smaller positive period works ($|\sin x|$ vanishes only at multiples of $\pi$).
am1-f-912 · level facile · notes
If the graph of $f$ passes through the point $(2, 5)$, through which point does the graph of $y = f(x) + 3$ pass?
  1. $(5, 5)$
  2. $(-1, 5)$
  3. $(2, 2)$
  4. $(2, 8)$
Explanation. $y = f(x) + a$ shifts the graph vertically by $a$: the abscissa stays $2$, the ordinate becomes $5 + 3 = 8$.
am1-f-913 · level facile · notes
If the graph of $f$ passes through the point $(2, 5)$, through which point does the graph of $y = f(x - 3)$ pass?
  1. $(-1, 5)$
  2. $(5, 5)$
  3. $(2, 2)$
  4. $(2, 8)$
Explanation. $y = f(x - 3)$ equals $5$ when $x - 3 = 2$, i.e. $x = 5$: a horizontal shift to the right by $3$.
am1-f-914 · level facile · notes
The graph of $y = \frac12 f(x)$ is obtained from that of $y = f(x)$ by:
  1. a horizontal stretch by a factor $2$
  2. a downward shift by $\frac12$
  3. a vertical compression by a factor $\frac12$
  4. a reflection in the $x$-axis
Explanation. $y = k\,f(x)$ multiplies the ordinates by $k$, leaving the abscissas: with $k = \frac12$ the graph is squeezed towards the $x$-axis, the zeros stay the same.
am1-f-915 · level facile · notes
What is the period of $y = f(3x)$ if $f$ is periodic with period $6$?
  1. $2$
  2. $18$
  3. $3$
  4. $6$
Explanation. $y = f(kx)$ compresses horizontally by a factor $k$: $f(3(x + 2)) = f(3x + 6) = f(3x)$, so the period becomes $\frac63 = 2$.
am1-f-916 · level ipotesi · notes
The graph of $y = |f(x)|$ is obtained by reflecting in the $x$-axis the parts where $f(x) < 0$. If $f(x) \ge 0$ for every $x$, what is obtained?
  1. the reflection of the graph of $f$ in the $x$-axis
  2. the reflection in the $y$-axis
  3. the same graph as $f$, because $|f(x)| = f(x)$
  4. a horizontal line
Explanation. The reflection concerns only the negative parts: if there are none, $|f(x)| = f(x)$ and the graph does not change.
am1-f-917 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = (x - 2)^2$. What is $f(|x|)$ at $x = -2$, and which symmetry does the graph of $y = f(|x|)$ have?
  1. $16$; symmetric about the $x$-axis
  2. $0$; symmetric about the $y$-axis
  3. $16$; no symmetry
  4. $0$; symmetric about the origin
Explanation. $f(|-2|) = f(2) = 0$. $f(|x|)$ is always an even function: keep the graph for $x \ge 0$ and reflect it in the $y$-axis.

10. Composite functions (13)

am1-f-053 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x^2$ and $g(x) = x + 1$, the composite function $g \circ f$ is:
  1. $(g \circ f)(x) = x^2 + 1$
  2. $(g \circ f)(x) = (x + 1)^2$
  3. $(g \circ f)(x) = x^2 + x$
  4. $(g \circ f)(x) = x^3 + x^2$
Explanation. $(g \circ f)(x) = g\big(f(x)\big)$: first apply $f$, then $g$. $(x+1)^2$ is $f \circ g$.
am1-f-054 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x - 3$ and $g(t) = \sqrt{t}$, for which $x$ is $g \circ f$ defined?
  1. for $x \ge 0$
  2. for $x \le 3$
  3. for every $x \in \R$
  4. for $x \ge 3$
Explanation. We need $f(x) \in [0, \ip)$, the domain of $g$: $x - 3 \ge 0$, i.e. $x \ge 3$.
am1-f-245 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = 2x + 1$ and $g(x) = x^2$, what is $(f \circ g)(3) - (g \circ f)(3)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $30$
  3. $-19$
  4. $-30$
Explanation. $(f \circ g)(3) = f(9) = 19$ and $(g \circ f)(3) = g(7) = 49$: the difference is $-30$. Composition is not commutative.
am1-f-246 · level facile · notes
The function $h(x) = \sqrt{\log x}$ (natural $\log$) is written as a composition $h = g \circ f$ with:
  1. $f(x) = \log x$, $g(t) = \sqrt t$
  2. $f(x) = \sqrt x$, $g(t) = \log t$
  3. $f(x) = x$, $g(t) = \sqrt{t}\,\log t$
  4. $f(x) = \log x$, $g(t) = t^2$
Explanation. $(g \circ f)(x) = g(f(x))$: the logarithm is applied first, then the root. The reverse order gives $\log\sqrt x$.
am1-f-247 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of $h(x) = \sqrt{\log x}$ ($\log$ the natural logarithm)?
  1. $(0, \ip)$
  2. $[1, \ip)$
  3. $(1, \ip)$
  4. $[0, \ip)$
Explanation. We need $x > 0$ for the logarithm and $\log x \ge 0$ for the root, i.e. $x \ge 1$.
am1-f-248 · level teorema · notes
Let $f: A \rr B$ and $g: B \rr C$. Which statement about the composition $g \circ f: A \rr C$ is correct?
  1. If $g \circ f$ is injective, then $g$ is injective.
  2. If $f$ and $g$ are increasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
  3. If $f$ and $g$ are injective, then $g \circ f$ is injective.
  4. If $f$ and $g$ are decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
Explanation. From $g(f(x_1)) = g(f(x_2))$ we get $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$ and then $x_1 = x_2$. The composition of two decreasing functions is increasing; if $g \circ f$ is injective it is $f$ (not $g$) that must be injective.
am1-f-249 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ for $x \neq 0$, what is $(f \circ f)(x)$?
  1. $\frac{1}{x^2}$
  2. $1$
  3. $x$
  4. $\frac{2}{x}$
Explanation. $f(f(x)) = \frac{1}{1/x} = x$ for $x \neq 0$: $f$ is its own inverse. $\frac{1}{x^2}$ is the product $f \cdot f$, not the composition.
am1-f-250 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x + 2$ and $g(x) = 3x$, what is the expression of $(g \circ f)(x)$?
  1. $3x + 2$
  2. $3x^2 + 6x$
  3. $x + 6$
  4. $3x + 6$
Explanation. $(g \circ f)(x) = g(x + 2) = 3(x + 2) = 3x + 6$. $3x + 2$ is $f \circ g$.
am1-f-520 · level facile · notes
With $f(x) = x^2$ and $g(x) = x - 1$, for which $x$ is $(f \circ g)(x) = (g \circ f)(x)$?
  1. for every $x \in \R$
  2. only for $x = 1$
  3. only for $x = 0$
  4. for no $x$
Explanation. $(f \circ g)(x) = (x - 1)^2 = x^2 - 2x + 1$ and $(g \circ f)(x) = x^2 - 1$: they are equal if $-2x + 1 = -1$, i.e. $x = 1$. In general composition is not commutative.
am1-f-521 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ and $g$ be real functions, with $g \circ f$ defined on an interval (“increasing” and “decreasing” in the strict sense). Which statement is correct?
  1. If $f$ and $g$ are decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
  2. If $f$ is increasing and $g$ is decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is increasing.
  3. If $f$ is increasing and $g$ is decreasing, then $g \circ f$ is decreasing.
  4. If $f$ and $g$ are increasing, then $g \circ f$ can be decreasing.
Explanation. If $x_1 < x_2$, then $f(x_1) < f(x_2)$ and, $g$ being decreasing, $g(f(x_1)) > g(f(x_2))$. Two decreasing functions instead give an increasing composite.
am1-f-522 · level facile · notes
What is the domain of $h(x) = \log(4 - x^2)$ (natural logarithm)?
  1. $[-2, 2]$
  2. $(-\infty, -2) \cup (2, \ip)$
  3. $(0, 2)$
  4. $(-2, 2)$
Explanation. $h = g \circ f$ with $f(x) = 4 - x^2$ and $g(t) = \log t$: we need $4 - x^2 > 0$, i.e. $-2 < x < 2$ (endpoints excluded).
am1-f-523 · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = 3x - 1$. Which function $g$ satisfies $(g \circ f)(x) = x$ for every $x \in \R$?
  1. $g(t) = \frac{t + 1}{3}$
  2. $g(t) = 3t + 1$
  3. $g(t) = \frac{t - 1}{3}$
  4. $g(t) = \frac{1}{3t - 1}$
Explanation. $g(f(x)) = \frac{(3x - 1) + 1}{3} = x$: $g$ is the inverse of $f$. The reciprocal $\frac{1}{3t - 1}$ is not the inverse.
am1-f-918 · level ipotesi · notes
The composition $g \circ f$, $(g \circ f)(x) = g(f(x))$, is defined at the points $x$ of the domain of $f$ for which $f(x)$ lies in the domain of $g$. With $f(x) = x - 5$ and $g(t) = \log t$, what is the domain of $g \circ f$?
  1. $\R$
  2. $(0, +\infty)$
  3. $(-5, +\infty)$
  4. $(5, +\infty)$
Explanation. We need $f(x) = x - 5 > 0$, i.e. $x > 5$: it is not enough that $x$ is in the domain of $f$ ($\R$), nor to take the domain of $g$.

11. Inverse functions (19)

am1-f-055 · level facile · notes
The arcsine is the function:
  1. $[-1, 1] \rr \left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
  2. $[-1, 1] \rr [0, \pi]$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
  3. $\R \rr \left(-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right)$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
  4. $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right] \rr [-1, 1]$, $y \mt \arcsin y$
Explanation. It is the inverse of the sine restricted to $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$: its domain is $[-1,1]$. $[0,\pi]$ is the range of the arccosine, $\R$ the domain of the arctangent.
am1-f-056 · level teorema · notes
Monotonicity and invertibility: which statement is correct for a function $f: D \rr \R$ with any $D \subseteq \R$?
  1. If $f$ is invertible on $D$, then it is strictly monotone on $D$.
  2. If $f$ is strictly increasing on $D$, then it is invertible and its inverse is strictly decreasing.
  3. If $f$ is strictly increasing (decreasing) on $D$, then it is invertible and its inverse is strictly increasing (decreasing).
  4. If $f$ is continuous on $D$, then it is invertible on $D$.
Explanation. Strict monotonicity implies injectivity and the inverse has the same monotonicity. The converse holds only for continuous functions on an interval; $\frac1x$ on $\R\setminus\{0\}$ is invertible but not monotone.
am1-f-057 · level facile · notes
The graph of $f^{-1}$ is obtained from that of $f$ by symmetry with respect to:
  1. the $y$-axis
  2. the line $y = x$
  3. the origin
  4. the $x$-axis
Explanation. The point $(x, y)$ lies on the graph of $f$ if and only if $(y, x)$ lies on the graph of $f^{-1}$: the coordinates are swapped.
am1-f-251 · level facile · notes
What is $\arccos\left(-\frac12\right)$?
  1. $-\frac\pi3$
  2. $\frac\pi3$
  3. $\frac{2\pi}{3}$
  4. $\frac{4\pi}{3}$
Explanation. The arccosine takes values in $[0, \pi]$: the only angle in $[0,\pi]$ with cosine $-\frac12$ is $\frac{2\pi}{3}$.
am1-f-252 · level facile · notes
What is $\arcsin\left(\sin\frac{3\pi}{4}\right)$?
  1. $\frac\pi4$
  2. $\frac{3\pi}{4}$
  3. $-\frac\pi4$
  4. $\frac{5\pi}{4}$
Explanation. $\sin\frac{3\pi}{4} = \frac{\sqrt2}{2}$ and the arcsine returns the angle in $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$ with that sine: $\frac\pi4$. $\arcsin(\sin x) = x$ only for $x \in \left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$.
am1-f-253 · level facile · notes
What is the inverse of the function $f: [0, \ip) \rr [0, \ip)$, $f(x) = x^4$?
  1. $f^{-1}(y) = y^{-4}$
  2. $f^{-1}(y) = \frac{y}{4}$
  3. $f^{-1}(y) = \pm\sqrt[4]{y}$
  4. $f^{-1}(y) = \sqrt[4]{y}$
Explanation. On $[0, \ip)$ the power $x^4$ is strictly increasing; its inverse is the arithmetic fourth root, with values $\ge 0$ (the $\pm$ sign does not give a function).
am1-f-254 · level facile · notes
What is the image of the arctangent function $\arctan: \R \rr \R$?
  1. $\left[-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right]$
  2. $\left(-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right)$
  3. $(0, \pi)$
  4. $\R$
Explanation. $\arctan$ is the inverse of the tangent restricted to $\left(-\frac\pi2, \frac\pi2\right)$; the values $\pm\frac\pi2$ are not attained (horizontal asymptotes).
am1-f-255 · level facile · notes
What is the inverse of the function $f: \R \rr (1, \ip)$, $f(x) = e^{x} + 1$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $f^{-1}(y) = \log y - 1$
  2. $f^{-1}(y) = \frac{1}{e^y + 1}$
  3. $f^{-1}(y) = \log(y - 1)$
  4. $f^{-1}(y) = e^{y - 1}$
Explanation. From $y = e^x + 1$ we get $e^x = y - 1$, hence $x = \log(y - 1)$, defined exactly for $y > 1$.
am1-f-524 · level facile · notes
Let $f: [0, 2] \rr [1, 5]$ be bijective with $f(2) = 5$, i.e. the point $(2, 5)$ lies on the graph of $f$. Which point lies on the graph of $f^{-1}$?
  1. $(2, 5)$
  2. $(-2, -5)$
  3. $(5, 2)$
  4. $\left(\frac12, \frac15\right)$
Explanation. $f^{-1}(5) = 2$: the graph of $f^{-1}$ is the mirror image of that of $f$ in the line $y = x$, which swaps the coordinates.
am1-f-525 · level facile · notes
On which set must $f(x) = x^2$ be restricted so that it is invertible with inverse $f^{-1}(y) = -\sqrt{y}$, $y \ge 0$?
  1. $[0, \ip)$
  2. $(-\infty, 0]$
  3. $\R$
  4. $[-1, 1]$
Explanation. On $(-\infty, 0]$ the function $x^2$ is strictly decreasing with image $[0, \ip)$, and the solution $x \le 0$ of $x^2 = y$ is $-\sqrt y$.
am1-f-526 · level facile · notes
What is the inverse of the hyperbolic sine $\sinH x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$, $x \in \R$ (natural $\log$)?
  1. $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 - 1}\right)$
  2. $\log\left(y + \sqrt{1 - y^2}\right)$
  3. $\frac{e^y + e^{-y}}{2}$
  4. $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$
Explanation. With $t = e^x$ the equation $\frac{t - t^{-1}}{2} = y$ becomes $t^2 - 2yt - 1 = 0$, with positive root $t = y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}$; hence $x = \log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$, defined for every $y \in \R$.
am1-f-527 · level teorema · notes
The hyperbolic cosine $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$ is not invertible on $\R$. Which restriction makes it invertible, and with which inverse (natural $\log$)?
  1. $[0, \ip)$, with inverse $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 - 1}\right)$, $y \ge 1$
  2. $\R$, with inverse $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$, $y \in \R$
  3. $[0, \ip)$, with inverse $\log\left(y + \sqrt{y^2 + 1}\right)$, $y \ge 0$
  4. $[1, \ip)$, with inverse $\log\left(y - \sqrt{y^2 - 1}\right)$, $y \ge 1$
Explanation. $\cosH$ is even, hence not injective on $\R$; on $[0, \ip)$ it is strictly increasing with image $[1, \ip)$. Solving $t^2 - 2yt + 1 = 0$ with $t = e^x \ge 1$ gives $t = y + \sqrt{y^2 - 1}$.
am1-f-919 · level ipotesi · notes
A strictly monotone function is invertible. Does the converse hold: is every invertible function $f: D \rr \R$ strictly monotone on $D$?
  1. yes, always
  2. no: $f(x) = \frac1x$ on $D = \R \setminus \{0\}$ is invertible but not monotone on $D$
  3. yes, if $D$ is any set of real numbers
  4. no, because monotone functions are not invertible
Explanation. $\frac1x$ is injective (it is its own inverse) but decreases on each half-line and $f(-1) < f(1)$: it is not monotone on the whole of $D$.
am1-f-920 · level ipotesi · notes
The theorem says that a STRICTLY increasing function is invertible. What happens if it is only non-decreasing, e.g. $f(x) = [x]$ (integer part)?
  1. it is still invertible, with a non-decreasing inverse
  2. it is invertible only on $\Z$
  3. it is invertible because it is monotone
  4. it is not injective ($f(0) = f(0.5) = 0$), hence not invertible
Explanation. With weak monotonicity the function can be constant on an interval and lose injectivity, which is necessary for invertibility.
am1-f-921 · level facile · notes
Let $f: \R \rr \R$ be strictly decreasing and bijective. What can be said about its inverse $f^{-1}$?
  1. it is strictly decreasing
  2. it is strictly increasing
  3. it is constant
  4. it is not monotone
Explanation. By the theorem on monotone functions the inverse has the same monotonicity: if $y_1 < y_2$ and $f^{-1}(y_1) < f^{-1}(y_2)$, applying $f$ would give $y_1 > y_2$.
am1-f-922 · level facile · notes
The graph of $f(x) = 2^x$ passes through $(3, 8)$. Through which point does the graph of its inverse $\log_2 x$ pass?
  1. $(3, \frac18)$
  2. $(-3, 8)$
  3. $(\frac18, 3)$
  4. $(8, 3)$
Explanation. The graph of the inverse is the reflection in the bisector $y = x$: coordinates are swapped, $\log_2 8 = 3$.
am1-f-923 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = x^3$ is a bijection of $\R$ onto $\R$. What is its inverse?
  1. $x^{-3}$
  2. $\frac{x}{3}$
  3. $\sqrt[3]{x}$, defined on all of $\R$
  4. $\sqrt[3]{x}$, defined only for $x \ge 0$
Explanation. For an odd exponent $x^3$ is increasing on $\R$ and the inverse is $\sqrt[3]{x}$, defined also for $x < 0$ ($\sqrt[3]{-8} = -2$). $x^{-3} = \frac{1}{x^3}$ is the reciprocal, not the inverse.
am1-f-924 · level facile · notes
The inverse hyperbolic cosine $\setcosH y$ is the inverse of $\cosH x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$ restricted to $[0, +\infty)$. What are its domain and image?
  1. domain $\R$, image $\R$
  2. domain $[1, +\infty)$, image $[0, +\infty)$
  3. domain $[0, +\infty)$, image $[1, +\infty)$
  4. domain $(-1, 1)$, image $\R$
Explanation. $\cosH$ on $[0, +\infty)$ is increasing with image $[1, +\infty)$; the inverse swaps domain and image. Domain $\R$ with image $\R$ belongs to $\setsinH$.
am1-f-929 · level facile · notes
A function $f: D \rr \R$ is invertible on $D$ if it is injective, i.e. $x_1 \neq x_2 \Rightarrow f(x_1) \neq f(x_2)$. Which function is invertible on its whole domain?
  1. $f(x) = x^2 - 2x$ on $\R$
  2. $f(x) = \cos x$ on $[0, 2\pi]$
  3. $f(x) = e^{-x}$ on $\R$
  4. $f(x) = |x|$ on $\R$
Explanation. $e^{-x}$ is strictly decreasing, hence injective. $x^2 - 2x$ is $0$ at $0$ and at $2$, $\cos 0 = \cos 2\pi$, $|-1| = |1|$.

Part: Limits (272)

1. Sequences and limits of sequences (34)

am1-f-058 · level teorema · notes
Which statement is the monotone sequence theorem?
  1. Every bounded sequence is convergent.
  2. Every non-decreasing sequence that is bounded above converges to $\sup\{a_n : n \in \N\}$.
  3. Every non-decreasing sequence that is bounded above converges to $\max\{a_n : n \in \N\}$.
  4. Every convergent sequence is monotone.
Explanation. The limit is the supremum, which need not be a maximum (e.g. $1 - \frac1n$). $(-1)^n$ is bounded but does not converge; $\frac{(-1)^n}{n}$ converges but is not monotone.
am1-f-059 · level facile · notes
The sequence $a_n = (-1)^n$ is:
  1. irregular (indeterminate)
  2. convergent to $0$
  3. divergent to $\ip$
  4. convergent to $1$
Explanation. It alternates between the values $1$ and $-1$: it neither converges nor diverges, so it is irregular.
am1-f-060 · level ipotesi · notes
Which argument proves that a convergent sequence $\{a_n\}$ has only one limit?
  1. the monotone sequence theorem: $\{a_n\}$ has a monotone subsequence, whose limit is the supremum
  2. the comparison theorem applied to the two constant sequences $\ell_1$ and $\ell_2$
  3. the Archimedean property and induction on $n$ in the definition of limit
  4. by contradiction, with two limits $\ell_1 \neq \ell_2$: the triangle inequality gives $|\ell_1 - \ell_2| < 2\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon > 0$
Explanation. From $|\ell_1 - \ell_2| \le |a_n - \ell_1| + |a_n - \ell_2| < 2\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon$ we get $\ell_1 = \ell_2$: a contradiction.
am1-f-256 · level facile · notes
Definition of limit: $a_n \rr \ell \in \R$ means that:
  1. there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $n$
  2. for every $\varepsilon > 0$ we have $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $n \in \N$
  3. for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $\nu$ such that $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$ for every $n > \nu$
  4. for every $n$ there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $|a_n - \ell| < \varepsilon$
Explanation. The distance from $\ell$ must become (eventually) smaller than any fixed $\varepsilon$. The second option asks too much (all $n$), the first and the last too little.
am1-f-257 · level facile · notes
For which $q \in \R$ does the geometric progression $a_n = q^n$ converge?
  1. $-1 < q \le 1$
  2. $|q| < 1$
  3. $|q| \le 1$
  4. $q > -1$
Explanation. $q^n \rr 0$ for $|q| < 1$ and $q^n = 1$ for $q = 1$; for $q > 1$ it diverges to $\ip$, for $q \le -1$ it is irregular (for $q = -1$ it oscillates between $\pm 1$).
am1-f-258 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about convergent sequences is correct?
  1. Every bounded sequence is convergent.
  2. Every convergent sequence is bounded.
  3. Every convergent sequence is monotone.
  4. Every convergent sequence has limit $0$.
Explanation. Eventually $|a_n - \ell| < 1$, and the first terms are finitely many: the sequence is bounded. The converse is false: $(-1)^n$ is bounded but does not converge.
am1-f-259 · level facile · notes
The sequence $a_n = \frac{n}{n + 1}$, $n \ge 0$, is:
  1. decreasing with limit $0$
  2. increasing and divergent
  3. irregular
  4. increasing with limit $1$
Explanation. $a_n = 1 - \frac{1}{n+1}$ increases with $n$ and tends to $1 = \sup\{a_n\}$, which is never attained.
am1-f-260 · level facile · notes
If $a_n \rr \ip$ and $a_n > 0$, the sequence $b_n = \frac{1}{a_n}$:
  1. diverges ($b_n \rr \ip$)
  2. is infinitesimal ($b_n \rr 0$)
  3. converges to $1$ ($b_n \rr 1$)
  4. may be irregular (no limit)
Explanation. The reciprocal of an infinite sequence is infinitesimal: for $\varepsilon > 0$, eventually $a_n > \frac1\varepsilon$, so $0 < b_n < \varepsilon$.
am1-f-528 · level facile · notes
For a non-empty set $E \subseteq \R$ one sets $\sup E = \ip$ if $E$ is not bounded above. What are $\sup E$ and $\inf E$ for $E = \{n^2 - 10n : n \in \N\}$?
  1. $\sup E = \ip$, $\inf E = \min E = -25$
  2. $\sup E = \ip$, $\inf E = \im$
  3. $\sup E = 0$, $\inf E = -25$
  4. $\sup E = \ip$, $\inf E = -24$
Explanation. $n^2 - 10n = (n - 5)^2 - 25 \ge -25$, with equality for $n = 5$: the minimum is $-25$. As $n \rr \ip$ the values grow without bound, so $\sup E = \ip$.
am1-f-529 · level facile · notes
With the convention $\inf E = \im$ for a set that is not bounded below, which statement about $E = \{x \in \R : x^3 < 1\}$ is correct?
  1. $\max E = 1$ and $\inf E = \im$
  2. $\sup E = 1$ and $\inf E = -1$
  3. $\sup E = 1$, which is not a maximum, and $\inf E = \im$
  4. $\sup E = \ip$ and $\inf E = \im$
Explanation. $x^3 < 1 \iff x < 1$, so $E = (-\infty, 1)$: $1$ is the supremum but does not belong to $E$, and $E$ is not bounded below.
am1-f-530 · level facile · notes
Which sequence is infinitesimal (i.e. tends to $0$ as $n \rr \ip$)?
  1. $a_n = (-1)^n$
  2. $a_n = \frac{(-1)^n}{n + 1}$
  3. $a_n = \frac{n}{n + 1}$
  4. $a_n = \log\frac{1}{n + 1}$
Explanation. $\left|\frac{(-1)^n}{n+1}\right| = \frac{1}{n+1} \rr 0$. $(-1)^n$ is irregular, $\frac{n}{n+1} \rr 1$ and $\log\frac{1}{n+1} = -\log(n+1) \rr \im$.
am1-f-531 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)^n$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\ip$
  3. $\im$
  4. it does not exist: the sequence is irregular
Explanation. For the geometric progression $q^n$ with $q < -1$ the terms alternate in sign and have modulus $|q|^n \rr \ip$: there is no limit.
am1-f-930 · level facile · notes
A sequence is a rule assigning to each $n \in \N$ (from some $n_0$ on) a real number $a_n$. For $a_n = \frac{(-1)^n}{n + 1}$, what is $a_3$?
  1. $\frac14$
  2. $-\frac14$
  3. $-\frac13$
  4. $\frac{1}{3}$
Explanation. Substitute $n = 3$: $a_3 = \frac{(-1)^3}{3 + 1} = -\frac14$.
am1-f-931 · level facile · notes
A sequence eventually has a property if there is $\tilde n$ such that $a_n$ satisfies it for every $n \ge \tilde n$. Which property does $a_n = n^2 - 10n$ eventually have?
  1. $a_n < 0$
  2. $a_n \le -25$
  3. $a_n > 100$
  4. $a_n = 0$
Explanation. $n^2 - 10n > 100$ for every $n \ge 18$ (for instance). $a_n < 0$ holds only for $1 \le n \le 9$, and $a_n = 0$ only for $n = 0$ and $n = 10$.
am1-f-932 · level facile · notes
Which sequence is eventually positive but NOT positive (i.e. $a_n > 0$ from some index on, but not for every $n \ge 0$)?
  1. $a_n = n - 5$
  2. $a_n = n^2 + 1$
  3. $a_n = (-1)^n$
  4. $a_n = -\frac{1}{n + 1}$
Explanation. $n - 5 > 0$ for $n \ge 6$, but $a_0 = -5$. $n^2 + 1$ is always positive, $(-1)^n$ changes sign forever, $-\frac{1}{n+1}$ is always negative.
am1-f-933 · level facile · notes
A sequence is non-negative if $a_n \ge 0$ for every $n$, positive if $a_n > 0$ for every $n$. How is $a_n = \frac{n}{n + 1}$, $n \ge 0$, classified?
  1. positive
  2. negative
  3. neither non-negative nor positive
  4. non-negative but not positive
Explanation. $a_0 = 0$ and $a_n > 0$ for $n \ge 1$: it is non-negative, not positive (although eventually positive).
am1-f-934 · level facile · notes
The sequence $a_n = \sqrt{n}$ diverges to $+\infty$: for every $M > 0$ there is $n(M)$ with $a_n > M$ for $n \ge n(M)$. Which $n(M)$ works?
  1. $n(M) = M$
  2. $n(M) = \sqrt{M}$
  3. $n(M) = [M^2] + 1$
  4. $n(M) = 2M$
Explanation. $\sqrt n > M \Leftrightarrow n > M^2$: $n \ge [M^2] + 1$ suffices. $n(M) = M$ or $2M$ fail for large $M$ ($\sqrt{2M} < M$ for $M > 2$).
am1-f-935 · level facile · notes
Which sequence diverges to $-\infty$ (for every $M > 0$, eventually $a_n < -M$)?
  1. $a_n = (-1)^n\,n$
  2. $a_n = 5 - n^2$
  3. $a_n = -\frac{1}{n + 1}$
  4. $a_n = -\sin n$
Explanation. $5 - n^2 < -M$ for $n > \sqrt{M + 5}$. $(-1)^n n$ is irregular (it oscillates), $-\frac1{n+1} \rr 0$, $-\sin n$ is bounded.
am1-f-936 · level facile · notes
For $\alpha \in \R$, what is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} n^\alpha$ in the three cases $\alpha > 0$, $\alpha = 0$, $\alpha < 0$?
  1. $+\infty$, $1$, $0$
  2. $+\infty$, $0$, $0$
  3. $+\infty$, $1$, $-\infty$
  4. $1$, $1$, $0$
Explanation. $n^\alpha$ grows without bound for $\alpha > 0$, is constantly $1$ for $\alpha = 0$, and $n^\alpha = \frac{1}{n^{|\alpha|}} \rr 0$ for $\alpha < 0$.
am1-f-937 · level facile · notes
In the extended reals $\R^* = \R \cup \{+\infty, -\infty\}$, which sequence has NO limit?
  1. $a_n = n^2$
  2. $a_n = -3n$
  3. $a_n = \frac{1}{n}$
  4. $a_n = (-2)^n$
Explanation. The first three have a limit in $\R^*$ ($+\infty$, $-\infty$, $0$). $(-2)^n$ takes alternately large positive and negative values: it is irregular.
am1-f-938 · level facile · notes
With the conventions $\sup E = +\infty$ ($\inf E = -\infty$) for sets not bounded above (below), what are $\sup$ and $\inf$ of $E = \{(-1)^n\,n : n \in \N\}$?
  1. $\sup E = +\infty$, $\inf E = 0$
  2. $\sup E = +\infty$, $\inf E = -\infty$
  3. $\sup E = 1$, $\inf E = -1$
  4. they do not exist
Explanation. Even $n$ give the values $0, 2, 4, \dots$, unbounded above, odd $n$ give $-1, -3, \dots$, unbounded below.
am1-f-939 · level facile · notes
What is the supremum of the sequence $a_n = 2 - \frac{1}{n}$, $n \ge 1$, i.e. $\sup\{a_n : n \ge 1\}$, and is it a maximum?
  1. $1$, and it is a maximum
  2. $+\infty$
  3. $2$, and it is a maximum
  4. $2$, but it is not a maximum
Explanation. $a_n < 2$ for every $n$ and $a_n \rr 2$: no number below $2$ is an upper bound, but $2$ is never attained. $1 = a_1$ is the minimum.
am1-f-940 · level teorema · notes
Theorem on infinitesimal sequences: which statement is correct?
  1. $a_n \rr \ell \Leftrightarrow |a_n| \rr |\ell|$ for every $\ell$
  2. $a_n \rr 0 \Rightarrow |a_n| \rr 1$
  3. $a_n \rr 0 \Leftrightarrow |a_n| \rr 0$
  4. $|a_n| \rr 0 \Rightarrow a_n \rr \ip$
Explanation. $|a_n - 0| = \big||a_n| - 0\big|$, so the two conditions coincide. For $\ell \neq 0$ only $\Rightarrow$ holds: $(-1)^n$ has $|a_n| \rr 1$ but does not converge.
am1-f-941 · level ipotesi · notes
We have $a_n \rr 0 \Leftrightarrow |a_n| \rr 0$. Does $|a_n| \rr 1 \Rightarrow a_n \rr 1$ (or $a_n \rr -1$) also hold?
  1. no: $a_n = (-1)^n$ has $|a_n| = 1$ but no limit
  2. yes, by the same theorem
  3. yes, provided $a_n$ is bounded
  4. no, because $|a_n|$ cannot tend to $1$
Explanation. The theorem works because the limit is $0$; with a non-zero limit the modulus “forgets” the sign, which may oscillate.
am1-f-942 · level facile · notes
A sequence tends to $\ell$ from above ($a_n \rr \ell^+$) if it tends to $\ell$ and eventually $a_n > \ell$. Which sequence tends to $0^+$?
  1. $a_n = -\frac1n$
  2. $a_n = \frac{(-1)^n}{n}$
  3. $a_n = 1 + \frac1n$
  4. $a_n = \frac{1}{n^2 + 1}$
Explanation. $\frac{1}{n^2+1} > 0$ and tends to $0$. $-\frac1n \rr 0^-$, $\frac{(-1)^n}{n} \rr 0$ changing sign (neither from above nor from below), $1 + \frac1n \rr 1^+$.
am1-f-943 · level facile · notes
The sequence $a_n = 3 - \frac{2}{n}$, $n \ge 1$, tends to $3$. From above or from below?
  1. from above, $3^+$
  2. from below, $3^-$
  3. neither from above nor from below
  4. it does not tend to $3$
Explanation. $a_n < 3$ for every $n$ and $a_n \rr 3$: limit from below, $\lim a_n = 3^-$.
am1-f-944 · level facile · notes
Is the sequence $a_n = n^2 - 6n$ eventually increasing? From which index?
  1. no, it is decreasing
  2. yes, it is increasing for every $n \ge 0$
  3. yes: $a_{n+1} - a_n = 2n - 5 > 0$ for $n \ge 3$
  4. yes, but only from $n = 6$ on
Explanation. $a_{n+1} - a_n = (n+1)^2 - 6(n+1) - n^2 + 6n = 2n - 5$, positive for $n \ge 3$: $a_3 = -9 < a_4 = -8 < \dots$; for $n \le 2$ it decreases.
am1-f-945 · level teorema · notes
Monotonicity theorem: if $\{a_n\}$ is non-decreasing and bounded above, what can be said?
  1. it converges to $\max\{a_n\}$, which always exists
  2. it converges to $\sup\{a_n : n \in \N\}$
  3. it diverges to $+\infty$
  4. it converges, but the limit may be smaller than some $a_n$
Explanation. The limit is the supremum of the values, which may not be attained ($1 - \frac1n \rr 1$). Every $a_n$ is $\le$ the limit.
am1-f-946 · level ipotesi · notes
The monotonicity theorem requires a monotone AND bounded sequence. Which example shows that boundedness alone does not give convergence?
  1. $a_n = 1 - \frac1n$
  2. $a_n = n$
  3. $a_n = \frac{1}{n}$
  4. $a_n = (-1)^n$
Explanation. $(-1)^n$ is bounded and irregular. $n$ is monotone but unbounded (and diverges); the other two are monotone, bounded and convergent.
am1-f-947 · level facile · notes
The sequence $a_n = \sum_{k=1}^{n} \frac{1}{k^2}$ is increasing and one proves that $a_n < 2$ for every $n$. What does the monotonicity theorem guarantee?
  1. that it converges to a limit $\ell \le 2$
  2. that it converges to $2$
  3. that it diverges
  4. nothing, because it is not decreasing
Explanation. Increasing and bounded above, hence convergent to its $\sup$, which is $\le 2$ (it is $\frac{\pi^2}{6} \approx 1.645$). The theorem does not say that the limit is the bound found.
am1-f-948 · level facile · notes
Corollary of the monotonicity theorem: a non-decreasing sequence always has a limit, equal to $\sup\{a_n\}$. What happens if it is not bounded above?
  1. it is irregular
  2. it diverges to $+\infty$
  3. it converges to $0$
  4. it diverges to $-\infty$
Explanation. For every $M$ there is $a_{\bar n} > M$ and, by monotonicity, $a_n \ge a_{\bar n} > M$ for $n \ge \bar n$: this is the definition of divergence to $+\infty$.
am1-f-949 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{2^n + 5^n}{4^n}$?
  1. $+\infty$
  2. $0$
  3. $\frac54$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\frac{2^n}{4^n} = \left(\frac12\right)^n \rr 0$ and $\frac{5^n}{4^n} = \left(\frac54\right)^n \rr +\infty$ (base $> 1$).
am1-f-950 · level ipotesi · notes
For the geometric progression $a^n$: $\lim a^n = 0$ if $|a| < 1$, $= 1$ if $a = 1$, $= +\infty$ if $a > 1$. What happens for $a = -1$?
  1. $a^n \rr 1$
  2. $a^n \rr 0$
  3. $a^n$ is irregular
  4. $a^n \rr -\infty$
Explanation. The case $a \le -1$ is excluded from the three limits: $(-1)^n$ oscillates between $\pm 1$, and for $a < -1$ it oscillates with growing modulus.
am1-f-951 · level facile · notes
How does the sequence $a_n = n\,(-1)^n + n$ behave?
  1. it diverges to $+\infty$
  2. it converges to $0$
  3. it is irregular
  4. it diverges to $-\infty$
Explanation. For odd $n$, $-n + n = 0$; for even $n$, $2n$: one subsequence stays at $0$, the other diverges, so there is no limit in $\R^*$.

2. Computing limits of sequences (21)

am1-f-061 · level teorema · notes
Which statement is the comparison (squeeze) theorem for sequences?
  1. If $a_n \rr \ell$, $c_n \rr \ell$ with $\ell \in \R$ and $a_n \le b_n \le c_n$ eventually, then $b_n \rr \ell$.
  2. If $a_n \rr \ell$ with $\ell \in \R$ and $a_n \le b_n$ eventually, then $b_n \rr \ell$.
  3. If $a_n \rr \ell_1$, $c_n \rr \ell_2$ and $a_n \le b_n \le c_n$, then $b_n \rr \frac{\ell_1 + \ell_2}{2}$.
  4. If $a_n \le b_n \le c_n$ eventually, then $\{b_n\}$ is convergent.
Explanation. One needs two sequences with the same finite limit squeezing $b_n$. A single lower bound is not enough: $0 \le (-1)^n + 1$ but $(-1)^n + 1$ does not converge.
am1-f-062 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{\sin n}{n}$?
  1. $1$
  2. it does not exist
  3. $0$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. $\{\sin n\}$ is bounded and $\frac1n \rr 0$: the product of a bounded sequence and an infinitesimal one tends to $0$ (corollary of the comparison theorem). The standard limit $\frac{\sin x}{x} \rr 1$ holds as $x \rr 0$, not here.
am1-f-063 · level ipotesi · notes
Sequences $\{c_n\}$ and $\{b_n\}$: which hypotheses suffice to conclude, with the comparison theorem, that $c_n\,b_n \rr 0$?
  1. $c_n \rr 0$ and $b_n \rr \ell \neq 0$
  2. $c_n \rr 0$ and $\{b_n\}$ bounded
  3. $c_n \rr 0$ and $b_n > 0$ for every $n$
  4. $\{c_n\}$ bounded and $b_n \rr \ell \in \R$
Explanation. It is enough that one sequence is infinitesimal and the other bounded. With $c_n \rr 0$ and $b_n = n$ (positive but unbounded) the product need not tend to $0$.
am1-f-261 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \frac{2n^2 - 3n}{5n^2 + 1}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-3$
  3. $\frac25$
  4. $\ip$
Explanation. Factor out $n^2$: $\frac{2 - \frac3n}{5 + \frac1{n^2}} \rr \frac25$. With numerator and denominator of the same degree the ratio of leading coefficients counts.
am1-f-262 · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{n \rr \ip} \left(\sqrt{n + 1} - \sqrt{n}\right)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $\ip$
  4. $\frac12$
