Explanation. Form $[\ip - \infty]$: multiplying and dividing by the sum of the roots gives $\frac{-3x - 3}{\sqrt{x^2 + x + 5} + \sqrt{x^2 + 4x + 8}}$, and the denominator is $\sim 2x$: the limit is $-\frac{3}{2}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-limite-radici · level media · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \left(\sqrt{x^2 - x + 8} - \sqrt{x^2 + 4x + 6}\right)$?
  1. $-\frac{5}{2}$
  2. $-5$
  3. $\frac{3}{2}$
  4. $0$
Explanation. Form $[\ip - \infty]$: multiplying and dividing by the sum of the roots gives $\frac{-5x + 2}{\sqrt{x^2 - x + 8} + \sqrt{x^2 + 4x + 6}}$, and the denominator is $\sim 2x$: the limit is $-\frac{5}{2}$.

3. Limits of polynomials and rational functions (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-limite-razionale · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \im} \frac{3x^2 + 3x + 1}{-4x^2 + x + 4}$?
  1. $+\infty$
  2. $\frac{1}{4}$
  3. $-\infty$
  4. $-\frac{3}{4}$
Explanation. Only the highest-degree terms matter: the limit is that of $\frac{3x^2}{-4x^2}$, i.e. $-\frac{3}{4}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-limite-razionale · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr \ip} \frac{-4x^2 + 4x + 3}{-x^3 + 2x^2 + 5x + 3}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $4$
  3. $+\infty$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Only the highest-degree terms matter: the limit is that of $\frac{-4x^2}{-x^3}$, i.e. $0$.
Example 1 · am1-g-asintoto-obliquo · level media · notes
What is the oblique asymptote of the function $f(x) = \frac{x^2 + 3x + 5}{x + 4}$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$?
  1. $y = x - 1$
  2. $y = x + 7$
  3. $y = x + 3$
  4. $y = x + 5$
Explanation. $m = \lim \frac{f(x)}{x} = 1$ and $q = \lim \big(f(x) - m\,x\big) = -1$ (equivalently, from dividing the numerator by $x + 4$): the asymptote is $y = x - 1$.
Example 2 · am1-g-asintoto-obliquo · level media · notes
What is the oblique asymptote of the function $f(x) = \frac{-x^2 + 4x - 2}{x + 4}$ as $x \rr \pm\infty$?
  1. $y = -x + 8$
  2. $y = -x - 2$
  3. $y = -x$
  4. $y = -x + 4$
Explanation. $m = \lim \frac{f(x)}{x} = -1$ and $q = \lim \big(f(x) - m\,x\big) = 8$ (equivalently, from dividing the numerator by $x + 4$): the asymptote is $y = -x + 8$.

6. Fundamental limits and asymptotic estimates (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-limite-notevole · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{e^{x} - 1}{3x}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $\frac{1}{3}$
  3. $3$
  4. $0$
Explanation. With the asymptotic estimates from the fundamental limits ($\sin t \sim t$, $\log(1+t) \sim t$, $e^t - 1 \sim t$, $1 - \cos t \sim \frac{t^2}{2}$ as $t \rr 0$) the limit is $\frac{1}{3}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-limite-notevole · level facile · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\log(1 + 4x)}{-2x}$?
  1. $-2$
  2. $2$
  3. $0$
  4. $-8$
Explanation. With the asymptotic estimates from the fundamental limits ($\sin t \sim t$, $\log(1+t) \sim t$, $e^t - 1 \sim t$, $1 - \cos t \sim \frac{t^2}{2}$ as $t \rr 0$) the limit is $-2$.
Example 1 · am1-g-limite-notevole-prodotto · level media · notes
Using the asymptotic estimates of the standard limits as $x \rr 0$ (natural $\log$), what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{(e^{x} - 1)\,\sin(2x)}{x\,\log(1 + 3x)}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $6$
  3. $\frac{1}{6}$
  4. $\frac{2}{3}$
Explanation. With $\sin t \sim t$, $\log(1 + t) \sim t$, $e^t - 1 \sim t$, $1 - \cos t \sim \frac{t^2}{2}$ (as $t \rr 0$) each factor is replaced by its equivalent: the limit is $\frac{2}{3}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-limite-notevole-prodotto · level media · notes
Using the asymptotic estimates of the standard limits as $x \rr 0$ (natural $\log$), what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\sin(2x)\,\log(1 - x)}{1 - \cos(x)}$?
  1. $-4$
  2. $0$
  3. $-1$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. With $\sin t \sim t$, $\log(1 + t) \sim t$, $e^t - 1 \sim t$, $1 - \cos t \sim \frac{t^2}{2}$ (as $t \rr 0$) each factor is replaced by its equivalent: the limit is $-4$.

8. Intermediate zero theorem and bisection method (1)

Example 1 · am1-g-bisezione · level complessa · notes
Bisection method for $f(x) = x^3 - 58$ on $[3, 4]$ (where $f$ changes sign): at each step evaluate $f$ at the midpoint and keep the half at whose endpoints $f$ has opposite signs. Which interval is obtained after $3$ steps?
  1. $[\frac{31}{8}, 4]$
  2. $[\frac{15}{4}, 4]$
  3. $[\frac{61}{16}, \frac{31}{8}]$
  4. $[\frac{15}{4}, \frac{31}{8}]$
Explanation. The successive intervals are $[\frac{7}{2}, 4]$, $[\frac{15}{4}, 4]$, $[\frac{15}{4}, \frac{31}{8}]$: each step halves the length, which is $\frac18$ after $3$ steps.
Example 2 · am1-g-bisezione · level complessa · notes
Bisection method for $f(x) = x^2 - 8$ on $[2, 3]$ (where $f$ changes sign): at each step evaluate $f$ at the midpoint and keep the half at whose endpoints $f$ has opposite signs. Which interval is obtained after $3$ steps?
  1. $[\frac{23}{8}, 3]$
  2. $[\frac{45}{16}, \frac{23}{8}]$
  3. $[\frac{11}{4}, 3]$
  4. $[\frac{11}{4}, \frac{23}{8}]$
Explanation. The successive intervals are $[\frac{5}{2}, 3]$, $[\frac{11}{4}, 3]$, $[\frac{11}{4}, \frac{23}{8}]$: each step halves the length, which is $\frac18$ after $3$ steps.

9. Weierstrass theorem and intermediate value theorem (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-weierstrass · level media · notes
What are the global maximum and minimum of $f(x) = x^3 - 3x - 4$ on the interval $[-3, 3]$?
  1. maximum $14$, minimum $-6$
  2. maximum $-2$, minimum $-6$
  3. maximum $14$, minimum $-22$
  4. maximum $-2$, minimum $-22$
Explanation. They exist by Weierstrass. Compare the values at the endpoints $f(-3) = -22$, $f(3) = 14$ and at the interior stationary points ($f'(x) = 3x^2 - 3$): maximum $14$, minimum $-22$.
Example 2 · am1-g-weierstrass · level media · notes
What are the global maximum and minimum of $f(x) = x^3 - 12x + 3$ on the interval $[-3, 3]$?
  1. maximum $12$, minimum $-13$
  2. maximum $12$, minimum $-6$
  3. maximum $19$, minimum $-13$
  4. maximum $19$, minimum $-6$
Explanation. They exist by Weierstrass. Compare the values at the endpoints $f(-3) = 12$, $f(3) = -6$ and at the interior stationary points ($f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12$): maximum $19$, minimum $-13$.
Example 1 · am1-g-immagine · level media · notes
By the Weierstrass and intermediate value theorems, the image of $[-2, 4]$ under the continuous function $f(x) = x^2 + 3$ is a closed interval. Which one?
  1. $[-19, -3]$
  2. $[7, 19]$
  3. $[-2, 4]$
  4. $[3, 19]$
Explanation. The minimum is at the vertex $x = 0$ (inside the interval), $f(0) = 3$; the maximum is at the endpoint farther from the vertex: $f(-2) = 7$, $f(4) = 19$. The image is $[3, 19]$.
Example 2 · am1-g-immagine · level media · notes
By the Weierstrass and intermediate value theorems, the image of $[-4, 1]$ under the continuous function $f(x) = x^2 + 2x - 4$ is a closed interval. Which one?
  1. $[-5, 4]$
  2. $[-4, 5]$
  3. $[-4, 1]$
  4. $[-1, 4]$
Explanation. The minimum is at the vertex $x = -1$ (inside the interval), $f(-1) = -5$; the maximum is at the endpoint farther from the vertex: $f(-4) = 4$, $f(1) = -1$. The image is $[-5, 4]$.

1. The derivative function (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-tangente · level facile · notes
What is the tangent line to the graph of $f(x) = x^3 + 3x^2 + 3x - 4$ at the point with abscissa $x_0 = 2$?
  1. $y = 27x - 32$
  2. $y = 13x - 4$
  3. $y = 27x + 22$
  4. $y = 27x + 76$
Explanation. $f(2) = 22$, $f'(2) = 27$; the tangent is $y = f(x_0) + f'(x_0)(x - x_0)$, i.e. $y = 27x - 32$.
Example 2 · am1-g-tangente · level facile · notes
What is the tangent line to the graph of $f(x) = 2x^3 + x^2 + 4x + 2$ at the point with abscissa $x_0 = 1$?
  1. $y = 12x + 9$
  2. $y = 12x - 3$
  3. $y = 12x + 21$
  4. $y = 7x + 2$
Explanation. $f(1) = 9$, $f'(1) = 12$; the tangent is $y = f(x_0) + f'(x_0)(x - x_0)$, i.e. $y = 12x - 3$.
Example 1 · am1-g-tangente-parallela · level facile · notes
At which point of the graph of $f(x) = x^2 + x + 3$ is the tangent line parallel to the line $y = 3x + 5$?
  1. $\left(3, 15\right)$
  2. $\left(2, 9\right)$
  3. $\left(-1, 3\right)$
  4. $\left(1, 5\right)$
Explanation. Parallel lines have the same slope: $f'(x) = 2x + 1 = 3$ gives $x = 1$, and $f(1) = 5$.
Example 2 · am1-g-tangente-parallela · level facile · notes
At which point of the graph of $f(x) = x^2 + 4x - 2$ is the tangent line parallel to the line $y = 2x - 5$?
  1. $\left(-1, -5\right)$
  2. $\left(1, 3\right)$
  3. $\left(3, 19\right)$
  4. $\left(-2, -6\right)$
Explanation. Parallel lines have the same slope: $f'(x) = 2x + 4 = 2$ gives $x = -1$, and $f(-1) = -5$.

4. Rules for computing derivatives (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-derivata · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = \frac{x + 3}{3x - 4}$. What is $f'(2)$?
  1. $\frac{5}{4}$
  2. $-\frac{13}{2}$
  3. $\frac{1}{3}$
  4. $-\frac{13}{4}$
Explanation. Using the quotient rule compute $f'(x)$ and evaluate it at $x = 2$: $f'(2) = -\frac{13}{4}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-derivata · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = \frac{3x - 2}{-3x - 1}$. What is $f'(2)$?
  1. $-\frac{9}{49}$
  2. $\frac{9}{49}$
  3. $\frac{3}{49}$
  4. $\frac{9}{7}$
Explanation. Using the quotient rule compute $f'(x)$ and evaluate it at $x = 2$: $f'(2) = -\frac{9}{49}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-derivata-composta · level media · notes
Let $f(x) = \sqrt{3x + 10}$. What is $f'(2)$?
  1. $\frac{3}{4}$
  2. $\frac{1}{8}$
  3. $3$
  4. $\frac{3}{8}$
Explanation. Chain rule: derivative of the outer function, evaluated at the inner function, times the derivative of the inner function. Evaluating at $x = 2$ gives $\frac{3}{8}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-derivata-composta · level media · notes
Let $f(x) = \sqrt{4x + 5}$. What is $f'(-1)$?
  1. $2$
  2. $-2$
  3. $4$
  4. $\frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. Chain rule: derivative of the outer function, evaluated at the inner function, times the derivative of the inner function. Evaluating at $x = -1$ gives $2$.

5. Mean value theorem, maxima and minima (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-estremi-locali · level media · notes
Let $f(x) = -2x^3 + 6x^2 + 3$. What are the local extrema of $f$?
  1. local maximum at $x = 2$ with value $8$; local minimum at $x = 0$ with value $0$
  2. local maximum at $x = 0$ with value $3$; local minimum at $x = 2$ with value $11$
  3. local maximum at $x = -2$ with value $43$; local minimum at $x = 0$ with value $3$
  4. local maximum at $x = 2$ with value $11$; local minimum at $x = 0$ with value $3$
Explanation. $f'(x) = -6x^2 + 12x = -6x(x - 2)$; from the sign of $f'$: local maximum at $x = 2$, local minimum at $x = 0$.
Example 2 · am1-g-estremi-locali · level media · notes
Let $f(x) = 2x^3 - 6x^2 - 18x - 2$. What are the local extrema of $f$?
  1. local maximum at $x = -1$ with value $8$; local minimum at $x = 3$ with value $-56$
  2. local maximum at $x = -1$ with value $10$; local minimum at $x = 3$ with value $-54$
  3. local maximum at $x = 1$ with value $-24$; local minimum at $x = -3$ with value $-56$
  4. local maximum at $x = 3$ with value $-56$; local minimum at $x = -1$ with value $8$
Explanation. $f'(x) = 6x^2 - 12x - 18 = 6(x + 1)(x - 3)$; from the sign of $f'$: local maximum at $x = -1$, local minimum at $x = 3$.
Example 1 · am1-g-monotonia · level media · notes
On which interval is the function $f(x) = x^3 - \frac{3}{2}x^2 - 6x + 3$ (strictly) decreasing (maximal interval)?
  1. $[-2, 1]$
  2. $(-\infty, -1] \cup [2, +\infty)$
  3. $[-1, 2]$
  4. $(-\infty, \frac{1}{2}]$
Explanation. $f'(x) = 3x^2 - 3x - 6 = 3(x + 1)(x - 2)$ is negative between the roots: by the monotonicity test $f$ is decreasing on $[-1, 2]$.
Example 2 · am1-g-monotonia · level media · notes
On which interval is the function $f(x) = x^3 + 9x^2 + 24x + 4$ (strictly) decreasing (maximal interval)?
  1. $[-4, -2]$
  2. $[2, 4]$
  3. $(-\infty, -4] \cup [-2, +\infty)$
  4. $(-\infty, -3]$
Explanation. $f'(x) = 3x^2 + 18x + 24 = 3(x + 4)(x + 2)$ is negative between the roots: by the monotonicity test $f$ is decreasing on $[-4, -2]$.

6. L'Hôpital's rule and differentiability (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-hopital-potenze · level media · notes
Using L'Hôpital's rule, what is $\lim_{x \rr -1} \frac{x^{4} - 1}{x^{5} + 1}$?
  1. $-1$
  2. $\frac{4}{5}$
  3. $-\frac{4}{5}$
  4. $-\frac{3}{4}$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$: the ratio of the derivatives is $\frac{4x^{3}}{5x^{4}}$, which at $x = -1$ equals $-\frac{4}{5}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-hopital-potenze · level media · notes
Using L'Hôpital's rule, what is $\lim_{x \rr 2} \frac{x^{5} - 32}{x^{3} - 8}$?
  1. $8$
  2. $4$
  3. $\frac{5}{3}$
  4. $\frac{20}{3}$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$: the ratio of the derivatives is $\frac{5x^{4}}{3x^{2}}$, which at $x = 2$ equals $\frac{20}{3}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-hopital-coseno · level media · notes
Using L'Hôpital's rule (twice if needed), what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{1 - \cos(3x)}{x\,\sin(x)}$?
  1. $\frac{3}{2}$
  2. $\frac{9}{2}$
  3. $9$
  4. $-\frac{9}{2}$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$; after two applications the ratio of the second derivatives at $0$ is $\frac{9}{2}$ (consistent with $1 - \cos t \sim \frac{t^2}{2}$ and $\sin t \sim t$): the limit is $\frac{9}{2}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-hopital-coseno · level media · notes
Using L'Hôpital's rule (twice if needed), what is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{1 - \cos(2x)}{x\,\sin(2x)}$?
  1. $-1$
  2. $2$
  3. $1$
  4. $\frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. Form $\left[\frac00\right]$; after two applications the ratio of the second derivatives at $0$ is $\frac{4}{4}$ (consistent with $1 - \cos t \sim \frac{t^2}{2}$ and $\sin t \sim t$): the limit is $1$.

7. Second derivative (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-flesso · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^3 + 5x^2 + 5x - 5$. What is the abscissa of the inflection point and where is $f$ convex?
  1. $x = -\frac{5}{3}$, convex for $x > -\frac{5}{3}$
  2. $x = \frac{5}{3}$, convex for $x > \frac{5}{3}$
  3. $x = -\frac{5}{3}$, convex for $x < -\frac{5}{3}$
  4. $x = -\frac{5}{6}$, convex for $x > -\frac{5}{6}$
Explanation. $f''(x) = 6x + 10$ vanishes at $x = -\frac{5}{3}$ and changes sign; it is positive (convexity) for $x > -\frac{5}{3}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-flesso · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = -x^3 + 6x^2 - 2x - 5$. What is the abscissa of the inflection point and where is $f$ convex?
  1. $x = 2$, convex for $x < 2$
  2. $x = 1$, convex for $x < 1$
  3. $x = -2$, convex for $x < -2$
  4. $x = 2$, convex for $x > 2$
Explanation. $f''(x) = -6x + 12$ vanishes at $x = 2$ and changes sign; it is positive (convexity) for $x < 2$.
Example 1 · am1-g-convessita · level complessa · notes
What are the inflection points of $f(x) = x^4 - 4x^3 + 4x$ and where is $f$ concave?
  1. inflections at $x = -2$ and $x = 0$; concave on $[-2, 0]$
  2. inflections at $x = 0$ and $x = 2$; concave on $(-\infty, 0] \cup [2, +\infty)$
  3. inflections at $x = 0$ and $x = 2$; concave on $[0, 2]$
  4. inflection at $x = 1$; concave on $(-\infty, 1]$
Explanation. $f''(x) = 12x^2 - 24x = 12x(x - 2)$: it is negative (concavity) between $0$ and $2$, positive outside, and changes sign at both roots, which are inflection points.
Example 2 · am1-g-convessita · level complessa · notes
What are the inflection points of $f(x) = x^4 - 2x^3 - 12x^2 - 2x$ and where is $f$ concave?
  1. inflection at $x = \frac{1}{2}$; concave on $(-\infty, \frac{1}{2}]$
  2. inflections at $x = -2$ and $x = 1$; concave on $[-2, 1]$
  3. inflections at $x = -1$ and $x = 2$; concave on $(-\infty, -1] \cup [2, +\infty)$
  4. inflections at $x = -1$ and $x = 2$; concave on $[-1, 2]$
Explanation. $f''(x) = 12x^2 - 12x - 24 = 12(x + 1)(x - 2)$: it is negative (concavity) between $-1$ and $2$, positive outside, and changes sign at both roots, which are inflection points.

8. Differential calculus and approximations (3)

Example 1 · am1-g-maclaurin · level facile · notes
What is the coefficient of $x^{4}$ in the Maclaurin polynomial of $f(x) = \cos(x)$?
  1. $\frac{1}{4}$
  2. $-\frac{1}{24}$
  3. $\frac{1}{24}$
  4. $1$
Explanation. The coefficient is $\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{4!}$; from the known expansion with $t = x$ we get $\frac{1}{24}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-maclaurin · level facile · notes
What is the coefficient of $x^{3}$ in the Maclaurin polynomial of $f(x) = \sin(3x)$?
  1. $-\frac{9}{2}$
  2. $-9$
  3. $\frac{9}{2}$
  4. $\frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. The coefficient is $\frac{f^{(3)}(0)}{3!}$; from the known expansion with $t = 3x$ we get $-\frac{9}{2}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-limite-sviluppi · level media · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\log(1 - x) + x}{-2x^2}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\frac{1}{4}$
  3. $-\frac{1}{8}$
  4. $-\frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. Expand the numerator with Maclaurin up to the first non-zero term, of degree $2$ (e.g. $e^t = 1 + t + \frac{t^2}{2} + o(t^2)$, $\sin t = t - \frac{t^3}{6} + o(t^3)$, $\log(1+t) = t - \frac{t^2}{2} + o(t^2)$, $\cos t = 1 - \frac{t^2}{2} + \frac{t^4}{24} + o(t^4)$ with $t = -x$): the limit is $\frac{1}{4}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-limite-sviluppi · level media · notes
What is $\lim_{x \rr 0} \frac{\sin(3x) - 3x}{2x^3}$?
  1. $-\frac{9}{4}$
  2. $\frac{1}{4}$
  3. $\frac{9}{2}$
  4. $0$
Explanation. Expand the numerator with Maclaurin up to the first non-zero term, of degree $3$ (e.g. $e^t = 1 + t + \frac{t^2}{2} + o(t^2)$, $\sin t = t - \frac{t^3}{6} + o(t^3)$, $\log(1+t) = t - \frac{t^2}{2} + o(t^2)$, $\cos t = 1 - \frac{t^2}{2} + \frac{t^4}{24} + o(t^4)$ with $t = 3x$): the limit is $-\frac{9}{4}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-maclaurin-prodotto · level complessa · notes
What is the Maclaurin polynomial of degree $3$ of $f(x) = e^{x}\,\cos(2x)$?
  1. $T_3(x) = \frac{1}{6}x^3 - \frac{3}{2}x^2 + x + 1$
  2. $T_3(x) = -\frac{23}{6}x^3 - \frac{3}{2}x^2 + x + 1$
  3. $T_3(x) = \frac{13}{6}x^3 + \frac{5}{2}x^2 + x + 1$
  4. $T_3(x) = -\frac{11}{6}x^3 - \frac{3}{2}x^2 + x + 1$
Explanation. Multiply the expansions $e^{t} = 1 + t + \frac{t^2}{2} + \frac{t^3}{6} + o(t^3)$ and $\cos u = 1 - \frac{u^2}{2} + o(u^3)$ (with $t = x$, $u = 2x$) and keep the terms up to $x^3$: $T_3(x) = -\frac{11}{6}x^3 - \frac{3}{2}x^2 + x + 1$.
Example 2 · am1-g-maclaurin-prodotto · level complessa · notes
What is the Maclaurin polynomial of degree $3$ of $f(x) = e^{2x}\,\sin(2x)$?
  1. $T_3(x) = -\frac{4}{3}x^3 + 4x^2 + 2x$
  2. $T_3(x) = \frac{16}{3}x^3 + 4x^2 + 2x$
  3. $T_3(x) = \frac{20}{3}x^3 + 4x^2 + 2x$
  4. $T_3(x) = \frac{8}{3}x^3 + 4x^2 + 2x$
Explanation. Multiply the expansions $e^{t} = 1 + t + \frac{t^2}{2} + \frac{t^3}{6} + o(t^3)$ and $\sin u = u - \frac{u^3}{6} + o(u^3)$ (with $t = 2x$, $u = 2x$) and keep the terms up to $x^3$: $T_3(x) = \frac{8}{3}x^3 + 4x^2 + 2x$.

9. Curve sketching (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-studio-razionale · level complessa · notes
Curve sketching: which description of $f(x) = \frac{x^2 + 8}{x - 1}$ is correct?
  1. vertical asymptote $x = 1$, oblique asymptote $y = x + 1$, local maximum at $x = 4$ with $f = 8$, local minimum at $x = -2$ with $f = -4$
  2. vertical asymptote $x = 1$, oblique asymptote $y = x - 1$, local maximum at $x = -2$ with $f = -4$, local minimum at $x = 4$ with $f = 8$
  3. vertical asymptote $x = 1$, oblique asymptote $y = x + 1$, local maximum at $x = -2$ with $f = -4$, local minimum at $x = 4$ with $f = 8$
  4. vertical asymptote $x = 1$, oblique asymptote $y = x + 1$, local maximum at $x = -2$ with $f = 8$, local minimum at $x = 4$ with $f = -4$
Explanation. $f(x) = x + 1 + \frac{9}{x - 1}$: vertical asymptote $x = 1$ and oblique $y = x + 1$. $f'(x) = 1 - \frac{9}{(x - 1)^2}$ vanishes at $x = 1 \pm 3$: local maximum at $-2$ ($f = -4$), local minimum at $4$ ($f = 8$). The local maximum is smaller than the local minimum.
Example 2 · am1-g-studio-razionale · level complessa · notes
Curve sketching: which description of $f(x) = \frac{x^2 + 8}{x + 1}$ is correct?
  1. vertical asymptote $x = -1$, oblique asymptote $y = x - 1$, local maximum at $x = -4$ with $f = 4$, local minimum at $x = 2$ with $f = -8$
  2. vertical asymptote $x = -1$, oblique asymptote $y = x - 1$, local maximum at $x = 2$ with $f = 4$, local minimum at $x = -4$ with $f = -8$
  3. vertical asymptote $x = -1$, oblique asymptote $y = x - 1$, local maximum at $x = -4$ with $f = -8$, local minimum at $x = 2$ with $f = 4$
  4. vertical asymptote $x = -1$, oblique asymptote $y = x + 1$, local maximum at $x = -4$ with $f = -8$, local minimum at $x = 2$ with $f = 4$
Explanation. $f(x) = x - 1 + \frac{9}{x + 1}$: vertical asymptote $x = -1$ and oblique $y = x - 1$. $f'(x) = 1 - \frac{9}{(x + 1)^2}$ vanishes at $x = -1 \pm 3$: local maximum at $-4$ ($f = -8$), local minimum at $2$ ($f = 4$). The local maximum is smaller than the local minimum.
Example 1 · am1-g-studio-cubica · level complessa · notes
Curve sketching: which description of $f(x) = x^3 - 3x^2 - 9x - 5$ is correct?
  1. local maximum at $x = -1$ ($f = 0$), local minimum at $x = 3$ ($f = -32$), inflection at $x = 1$, 2 distinct real zeros
  2. local maximum at $x = 3$ ($f = -32$), local minimum at $x = -1$ ($f = 0$), inflection at $x = 1$, 2 distinct real zeros
  3. local maximum at $x = -1$ ($f = 0$), local minimum at $x = 3$ ($f = -32$), inflection at $x = 1$, 3 distinct real zeros
  4. local maximum at $x = -1$ ($f = 0$), local minimum at $x = 3$ ($f = -32$), inflection at $x = -1$, 2 distinct real zeros
Explanation. $f'(x) = 3(x - 1)^2 - 12$ vanishes at $x = 1 \pm 2$; $f''(x) = 6(x - 1)$ gives the inflection at $x = 1$. Values: $f(-1) = 0$ (maximum), $f(3) = -32$ (minimum). By the intermediate zero theorem and monotonicity on the intervals there are 2 real zeros.
Example 2 · am1-g-studio-cubica · level complessa · notes
Curve sketching: which description of $f(x) = x^3 + 3x^2 - 9x - 19$ is correct?
  1. local maximum at $x = -3$ ($f = 8$), local minimum at $x = 1$ ($f = -24$), inflection at $x = 1$, 3 distinct real zeros
  2. local maximum at $x = 1$ ($f = -24$), local minimum at $x = -3$ ($f = 8$), inflection at $x = -1$, 3 distinct real zeros
  3. local maximum at $x = -3$ ($f = 8$), local minimum at $x = 1$ ($f = -24$), inflection at $x = -1$, exactly one real zero
  4. local maximum at $x = -3$ ($f = 8$), local minimum at $x = 1$ ($f = -24$), inflection at $x = -1$, 3 distinct real zeros
Explanation. $f'(x) = 3(x + 1)^2 - 12$ vanishes at $x = -1 \pm 2$; $f''(x) = 6(x + 1)$ gives the inflection at $x = -1$. Values: $f(-3) = 8$ (maximum), $f(1) = -24$ (minimum). By the intermediate zero theorem and monotonicity on the intervals there are 3 real zeros.

10. Newton's method (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-newton · level media · notes
Newton's method $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ for $f(x) = x^{3} - 24$, with $x_0 = 3$: what is $x_1$?
  1. $\frac{8}{3}$
  2. $\frac{28}{9}$
  3. $\frac{26}{9}$
  4. $-78$
Explanation. $f(3) = 3$ and $f'(x) = 3x^{2}$, so $f'(3) = 27$ and $x_1 = 3 - \frac{3}{27} = \frac{26}{9}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-newton · level media · notes
Newton's method $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ for $f(x) = x^{2} - 11$, with $x_0 = 4$: what is $x_1$?
  1. $-36$
  2. $\frac{11}{4}$
  3. $\frac{37}{8}$
  4. $\frac{27}{8}$
Explanation. $f(4) = 5$ and $f'(x) = 2x^{1}$, so $f'(4) = 8$ and $x_1 = 4 - \frac{5}{8} = \frac{27}{8}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-newton-due-passi · level complessa · notes
Newton's method $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ for $f(x) = x^2 - 12$ (zero $\sqrt{12}$), with $x_0 = 3$: what is $x_2$?
  1. $\frac{7}{2}$
  2. $\frac{97}{28}$
  3. $\frac{27}{20}$
  4. $\frac{13}{4}$
Explanation. Here $x_{n+1} = \frac12\left(x_n + \frac{12}{x_n}\right)$: $x_1 = \frac{7}{2}$ and $x_2 = \frac{97}{28}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-newton-due-passi · level complessa · notes
Newton's method $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ for $f(x) = x^2 - 7$ (zero $\sqrt{7}$), with $x_0 = 1$: what is $x_2$?
  1. $\frac{23}{8}$
  2. $\frac{5}{2}$
  3. $-\frac{5}{4}$
  4. $4$
Explanation. Here $x_{n+1} = \frac12\left(x_n + \frac{7}{x_n}\right)$: $x_1 = 4$ and $x_2 = \frac{23}{8}$.

1. Numerical series (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-serie-geometrica · level facile · notes
What is the sum of the series $\sum_{k=2}^{\infty} 2\,\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^k$?
  1. $\frac{1}{24}$
  2. $\frac{1}{6}$
  3. $\frac{2}{3}$
  4. $\frac{1}{10}$
Explanation. Geometric series with ratio $q = \frac{1}{4}$, $|q| < 1$: $\sum_{k=2}^{\infty} q^k = \frac{q^{2}}{1 - q}$; the sum is $\frac{1}{6}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-serie-geometrica · level facile · notes
What is the sum of the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} 2\,\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^k$?
  1. $6$
  2. $\frac{6}{7}$
  3. $8$
  4. $\frac{9}{2}$
Explanation. Geometric series with ratio $q = \frac{3}{4}$, $|q| < 1$: $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} q^k = \frac{q^{1}}{1 - q}$; the sum is $6$.
Example 1 · am1-g-serie-telescopica · level media · notes
What is the sum of the telescoping series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{4}{(k + 2)(k + 4)}$?
  1. $\frac{7}{3}$
  2. $\frac{2}{3}$
  3. $\frac{7}{6}$
  4. $\frac{4}{3}$
Explanation. $\frac{1}{(k + 2)(k + 4)} = \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{k + 2} - \frac{1}{k + 4}\right)$: in the partial sums only the first $2$ positive terms survive, $\frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{4}$, multiplied by $\frac{4}{2}$. The sum is $\frac{7}{6}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-serie-telescopica · level media · notes
What is the sum of the telescoping series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{1}{(k + 1)(k + 4)}$?
  1. $\frac{1}{2}$
  2. $\frac{13}{12}$
  3. $\frac{1}{6}$
  4. $\frac{13}{36}$
Explanation. $\frac{1}{(k + 1)(k + 4)} = \frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{1}{k + 1} - \frac{1}{k + 4}\right)$: in the partial sums only the first $3$ positive terms survive, $\frac{1}{2} + \dots + \frac{1}{4}$, multiplied by $\frac{1}{3}$. The sum is $\frac{13}{36}$.

2. Series with non-negative terms (3)

Example 1 · am1-g-serie-parametro · level media · notes
For which $\alpha \in \R$ does the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{\sqrt{k^{3} + 5}}{k^{\alpha}\,(k^{2} + 1)}$ converge?
  1. converges if and only if $\alpha > \frac{1}{2}$
  2. converges if and only if $\alpha > -\frac{1}{2}$
  3. converges if and only if $\alpha \ge \frac{1}{2}$
  4. converges if and only if $\alpha < \frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. Positive terms and $a_k \sim \frac{k^{\frac{3}{2}}}{k^{\alpha + 2}} = \frac{1}{k^{\alpha + \frac{1}{2}}}$: by limit comparison with the generalized harmonic series it converges iff $\alpha + \frac{1}{2} > 1$, i.e. $\alpha > \frac{1}{2}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-serie-parametro · level media · notes
For which $\alpha \in \R$ does the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{k^{2} + 1}{k^{\alpha}\,(k^{3} + 2)}$ converge?
  1. converges if and only if $\alpha > 0$
  2. converges if and only if $\alpha \ge 0$
  3. converges if and only if $\alpha > -1$
  4. converges if and only if $\alpha < 0$
Explanation. Positive terms and $a_k \sim \frac{k^{2}}{k^{\alpha + 3}} = \frac{1}{k^{\alpha + 1}}$: by limit comparison with the generalized harmonic series it converges iff $\alpha + 1 > 1$, i.e. $\alpha > 0$.
Example 1 · am1-g-criterio-rapporto · level media · notes
Ratio test for the positive series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} a_k$ with $a_k = \frac{8^k}{k!}$: what is $\ell = \lim_{k \rr \ip} \frac{a_{k+1}}{a_k}$ and what is concluded?
  1. $\ell = 1$: the test is inconclusive
  2. $\ell = 8$: the series diverges
  3. $\ell = 0$: the series converges
  4. $\ell = 0$: the series diverges
Explanation. Compute $\frac{a_{k+1}}{a_k}$ and take its limit: $\ell = 0$. If $\ell < 1$ the series converges, if $\ell > 1$ (or $\ell = \ip$) it diverges, if $\ell = 1$ the test is inconclusive.
Example 2 · am1-g-criterio-rapporto · level media · notes
Ratio test for the positive series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} a_k$ with $a_k = \frac{4^k}{k!}$: what is $\ell = \lim_{k \rr \ip} \frac{a_{k+1}}{a_k}$ and what is concluded?
  1. $\ell = 0$: the series diverges
  2. $\ell = 1$: the test is inconclusive
  3. $\ell = 4$: the series diverges
  4. $\ell = 0$: the series converges
Explanation. Compute $\frac{a_{k+1}}{a_k}$ and take its limit: $\ell = 0$. If $\ell < 1$ the series converges, if $\ell > 1$ (or $\ell = \ip$) it diverges, if $\ell = 1$ the test is inconclusive.
Example 1 · am1-g-carattere-serie · level facile · notes
How does the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{4k^{2}}{k^{4} + 6}$ behave?
  1. it converges
  2. it diverges to $-\infty$
  3. it diverges to $+\infty$
  4. it is irregular
Explanation. The general term has constant sign and is asymptotic to $4\,\frac{1}{k^{2}}$: by asymptotic comparison the series behaves like the generalised harmonic series $\sum \frac{1}{k^{2}}$, which converges if and only if the exponent is $> 1$.
Example 2 · am1-g-carattere-serie · level facile · notes
How does the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{2k}{k^{4} + 1}$ behave?
  1. it converges
  2. it diverges to $+\infty$
  3. it diverges to $-\infty$
  4. it is irregular
Explanation. The general term has constant sign and is asymptotic to $2\,\frac{1}{k^{3}}$: by asymptotic comparison the series behaves like the generalised harmonic series $\sum \frac{1}{k^{3}}$, which converges if and only if the exponent is $> 1$.

3. Series with terms of variable sign (2)

Example 1 · am1-g-leibniz-stima · level media · notes
For the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k^{2}}$, with sum $s$ and partial sums $s_n$, the Leibniz estimate is $|s - s_n| \le a_{n+1}$ with $a_k = \frac{1}{k^{2}}$. What is the smallest $n$ guaranteeing $|s - s_n| \le 10^{-4}$?
  1. $99$
  2. $100$
  3. $101$
  4. $9999$
Explanation. We need $\frac{1}{(n+1)^{2}} \le 10^{-4}$, i.e. $n + 1 \ge 10^{2} = 100$: the smallest is $n = 99$.
Example 2 · am1-g-leibniz-stima · level media · notes
For the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k^{2}}$, with sum $s$ and partial sums $s_n$, the Leibniz estimate is $|s - s_n| \le a_{n+1}$ with $a_k = \frac{1}{k^{2}}$. What is the smallest $n$ guaranteeing $|s - s_n| \le 10^{-2}$?
  1. $10$
  2. $99$
  3. $9$
  4. $11$
Explanation. We need $\frac{1}{(n+1)^{2}} \le 10^{-2}$, i.e. $n + 1 \ge 10^{1} = 10$: the smallest is $n = 9$.
Example 1 · am1-g-convergenza-alternata · level complessa · notes
How does the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} (-1)^k\,\frac{k^{2}}{k^{3} + 1}$ behave?
  1. it converges absolutely
  2. it converges, but not absolutely
  3. it does not converge: the general term does not tend to $0$
  4. it does not converge, although the general term tends to $0$
Explanation. The modulus of the general term is $\sim \frac{1}{k}$. If the exponent is $> 1$ the series of moduli converges; if it is in $(0, 1]$ it converges only by Leibniz (eventually decreasing infinitesimal terms); if it is $\le 0$ the general term does not tend to $0$.
Example 2 · am1-g-convergenza-alternata · level complessa · notes
How does the series $\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} (-1)^k\,\frac{k}{k^{5} + 1}$ behave?
  1. it does not converge, although the general term tends to $0$
  2. it does not converge: the general term does not tend to $0$
  3. it converges absolutely
  4. it converges, but not absolutely
Explanation. The modulus of the general term is $\sim \frac{1}{k^{4}}$. If the exponent is $> 1$ the series of moduli converges; if it is in $(0, 1]$ it converges only by Leibniz (eventually decreasing infinitesimal terms); if it is $\le 0$ the general term does not tend to $0$.

4. Series of functions (1)

Example 1 · am1-g-somma-taylor · level facile · notes
Using the Taylor series $e^x = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{x^k}{k!}$, valid for every $x \in \R$, what is $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{2^k}{k!}$?
  1. $e^{2} - 1$
  2. $e^{2}$
  3. $e^{-2}$
  4. $e^{-2} - 1$
Explanation. With $x = 2$ the series from $k = 0$ equals $e^{2}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-somma-taylor · level facile · notes
Using the Taylor series $e^x = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{x^k}{k!}$, valid for every $x \in \R$, what is $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{(-2)^k}{k!}$?
  1. $e^{2} - 1$
  2. $e^{2}$
  3. $e^{-2}$
  4. $e^{-2} - 1$
Explanation. With $x = -2$ the series from $k = 0$ equals $e^{-2}$.

Antiderivatives (1)

Example 1 · am1-g-primitiva-valore · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the antiderivative of $f(x) = 6x^2 + 4x + 3$ with $F(0) = 4$. What is $F(-3)$?
  1. $-41$
  2. $-131$
  3. $-45$
  4. $-28$
Explanation. The antiderivatives are $2x^3 + 2x^2 + 3x + C$; the condition $F(0) = 4$ gives $C = 4$, so $F(-3) = -41$.
Example 2 · am1-g-primitiva-valore · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the antiderivative of $f(x) = -3x^2 - 4x - 2$ with $F(0) = -4$. What is $F(-1)$?
  1. $-3$
  2. $1$
  3. $-2$
  4. $-7$
Explanation. The antiderivatives are $-x^3 - 2x^2 - 2x + C$; the condition $F(0) = -4$ gives $C = -4$, so $F(-1) = -3$.

Integrals (4)

Example 1 · am1-g-integrale · level facile · notes
What is $\int_{-2}^{3} \left(x^2 + 3x + 3\right) dx$?
  1. $\frac{173}{6}$
  2. $65$
  3. $-\frac{205}{6}$
  4. $\frac{205}{6}$
Explanation. An antiderivative is $F(x) = \frac{1}{3}x^3 + \frac{3}{2}x^2 + 3x$ and the integral equals $F(3) - F(-2) = \frac{205}{6}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-integrale · level facile · notes
What is $\int_{-2}^{0} \left(-x^2 + 3x - 2\right) dx$?
  1. $-\frac{38}{3}$
  2. $\frac{38}{3}$
  3. $-24$
  4. $10$
Explanation. An antiderivative is $F(x) = -\frac{1}{3}x^3 + \frac{3}{2}x^2 - 2x$ and the integral equals $F(0) - F(-2) = -\frac{38}{3}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-area · level media · notes
What is the area of the bounded region between the parabola $y = x^2 + 2x + 3$ and the line $y = 5x + 3$?
  1. $-\frac{9}{2}$
  2. $\frac{9}{2}$
  3. $9$
  4. $\frac{27}{2}$
Explanation. The curves meet at $x = 0$ and $x = 3$; between them the line lies above and the area is $\int_{0}^{3} -(x)(x - 3)\,dx = \frac{3^3}{6} = \frac{9}{2}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-area · level media · notes
What is the area of the bounded region between the parabola $y = x^2 - x - 4$ and the line $y = 2x$?
  1. $\frac{125}{6}$
  2. $\frac{125}{3}$
  3. $\frac{125}{2}$
  4. $-\frac{125}{6}$
Explanation. The curves meet at $x = -1$ and $x = 4$; between them the line lies above and the area is $\int_{-1}^{4} -(x + 1)(x - 4)\,dx = \frac{5^3}{6} = \frac{125}{6}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-integrale-trig · level facile · notes
What is $\int_0^{\frac{\pi}{3}} \big(\sin(3x) + \cos(3x)\big)\,dx$?
  1. $-3$
  2. $2$
  3. $6$
  4. $\frac{2}{3}$
Explanation. An antiderivative is $-\frac{1}{3}\cos(3x) + \frac{1}{3}\sin(3x)$: evaluating it between $0$ and $\frac{\pi}{3}$ gives $\frac{2}{3}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-integrale-trig · level facile · notes
What is $\int_0^{\frac{\pi}{3}} \big(-\sin(3x) + 4\cos(3x)\big)\,dx$?
  1. $-\frac{2}{3}$
  2. $3$
  3. $-6$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. An antiderivative is $-\frac{-1}{3}\cos(3x) + \frac{4}{3}\sin(3x)$: evaluating it between $0$ and $\frac{\pi}{3}$ gives $-\frac{2}{3}$.
Example 1 · am1-g-area-tra-curve · level complessa · notes
What is the area of the bounded region of the plane enclosed by the parabola $y = -2x^2 + 7x + 2$ and the line $y = 3x - 4$?
  1. $\frac{64}{3}$
  2. $\frac{128}{3}$
  3. $128$
  4. $16$
Explanation. The curves meet where $-2x^2 + 4x + 6 = 0$, i.e. at $x = -1$ and $x = 3$. The area is $\int_{-1}^{3} \big|-2\,(x + 1)(x - 3)\big|\,dx = \frac{2 \cdot 4^3}{6} = \frac{64}{3}$.
Example 2 · am1-g-area-tra-curve · level complessa · notes
What is the area of the bounded region of the plane enclosed by the parabola $y = -x^2 - x + 7$ and the line $y = -3x - 1$?
  1. $36$
  2. $216$
  3. $72$
  4. $18$
Explanation. The curves meet where $-x^2 + 2x + 8 = 0$, i.e. at $x = -2$ and $x = 4$. The area is $\int_{-2}^{4} \big|-1\,(x + 2)(x - 4)\big|\,dx = \frac{1 \cdot 6^3}{6} = 36$.
